§1 The honest bottom line — 2nd edition, read this first

This edition adds three deep new investigations you asked for — can we catch the big moves we miss?, if not, what signal would?, and where do passive limit orders actually work? — each given the same 7-contract forensic treatment and each claim re-run by an independent skeptic. Here is what survived that fight. It's sobering, it's honest, and it points somewhere real.

§1.1 The three new investigations — what they proved

1. Can our idle gates catch the big misses? No. (Chapter: How We Catch the Big Misses.) Fired mechanically across the whole week, momentum, dip and ORB lose on every contract — they're faders and knife-catchers getting shredded chasing. That's not a failure, it's a clean result: the desk is right to refuse these moves with the tools it has.

2. If not, what signal would? Mostly nothing — with one exception. (Chapter: Signal Discovery.) We dissected the tape before each big move and designed new signals from scratch. Three candidates surfaced; the skeptic killed two as curve-fits (MNQ's £998 "thrust-continuation" collapsed to £197 on re-run; M2K's wasn't durable). The uncomfortable truth: MES and MGC's biggest moves are silent — they ignite on below-average volume with no book fingerprint, so nothing in our captured data predicts them. One new edge held: MBT's thin-book spike-fade (below).

3. Where do passive limit orders work? Nowhere yet — and now we know exactly why. (Chapter: Passive Limit Orders.) Passive chop is +$10k on paper, ~−$10k on the real tape. The mechanism is adverse selection, proven cold: every passive fill was a "through-fill" — you only get filled when price trades through your limit (you were wrong and it kept going), while the clean bounces that would pay never touch your order. You keep the losers and miss the winners. No condition band rescues it on any contract.

§1.3 The two infrastructure unlocks — worth more than any single edge

This is where the real money probably is
A. Capture the aggressor tape. Three agents independently hit the same wall: MES/MGC's big moves are silent in everything we record (price, volume, quotes). The one thing that would reveal who's leaning into them before price moves is the true buy/sell print (reqTickByTick AllLast) — which we don't capture. This is the highest-value data upgrade on the desk. B. Upgrade the passive repricer. It only ever tested an at-touch limit (offset 0.0). To know if resting deeper escapes the toxic through-fills, the repricer needs to model multiple limit offsets. Until it does, "passive is dead" is only proven at the touch.

§1.4 So how do we make money — the honest plan

The plan that follows from the proof
1. Fix the two bugs live. 2. Shadow-arm the two survivors (ride-strength, MBT spike-fade) and let them prove out of sample. 3. Don't run passive; don't chase the silent moves with the gates we have. 4. Build the aggressor-tape capture — it's the thing most likely to unlock the big directional moves we're currently blind to. 5. Arm nothing else live until it survives a second week. This week's three deep labs found mostly honest nulls — and a null that stops us lighting money on fire is a result, not a disappointment.

Every section carries a stamp: 🟢 verified / candidate-found, 🟠 refuted, or ◦ honest-null. From now on these three chapters are standing — every Friday runs them, and as the weeks accrue the job becomes tuning the shadows, not adding studies.

§2 The Big Flip — ride strength instead of fading it

This week the desk kept selling into things that were shooting up and buying things that were flushing down — standing in front of the train. On the 75 trades where the market had just surged hard, that fade cost us −$490. This section takes those exact same entry moments and asks one question: what if we had turned around and gone with the move for ten minutes? The answer, proven trade-by-trade on this week's 5-second bars, is +$758. That is a ~$1,250 swing on one week — the single biggest money idea in this report. But it is not "flip everything", and it is not the same for every contract. Here is the proof, and the honest small print.

−$490
desk P&L, 75 strong-surge trades
+$758
same entries, ridden 10 min
~$1,250
weekly swing from the flip
61%
win rate riding (46W / 29L)

§2.1 What "fading strength" actually looked like

The two live gates (vwap_pullback and vwap_dip) are built to fade: they wait for a strong push, then bet it snaps back. That works on a quiet, drifting tape. It is a disaster when the push is a real, violent move — a big seller flushing the book, or a squeeze that just keeps going. Our own memory of the week says it plainly: 43 of 63 losses were fading a strong move that kept going; the winners were gentle nudges (net_atr ~0.14), the losers were freight trains (net_atr ~0.31).

To measure the surge honestly I did not trust the gate's own label. For every trade I went to the raw 5-second bars and defined a move as "strong" when the desk's move-size reading net_atr > 0.30 (the last leg travelled more than a third of an ATR) or ATR was actively expanding (atr_expanding = true) — i.e. volatility bursting open, not settling. That splits the week's 137 futures trades (MNQ, MES, MGC — the only three that traded live) cleanly in two:

BucketnDesk actualFlip & ride 10 minRide win%Read
STRONG (net_atr>0.30 or ATR expanding)75−$490+$75861%Fading a freight train. The flip pays.
GENTLE (everything else)62−$341+$1745%Riding does nothing here. Different problem.
THE KEY SEPARATION
This is what makes it a real edge and not a lazy "we had the sign backwards". Flipping the gentle trades earns essentially zero (+$17, 45% win) — those losses are a different disease (chop, over-trading, exits). The riding edge lives only in the strong-surge bucket, where going with the move flips −$490 into +$758. Strength is the trigger, not sign-error.

§2.2 Before / after — the same week, two ways

Both lines below are the same 75 strong-surge trades in the order they happened (Mon 29 Jun → Fri 3 Jul). Red is what the desk actually banked, running down to −$490. Green is what those identical entries made if we'd turned around and ridden the move for ten minutes, net of a full tick of spread and $1.24 round-turn commission. Notice where they tear apart: Monday afternoon (29 Jun, ~13:40–19:00 UTC), when MNQ went into an expanding-ATR flush and the desk kept buying the dip.

Cumulative $ over the 75 strong-surge trades in time order. ▬ red = desk actual (→ −$490)   ▬ green = flip & ride 10 min (→ +$758). Dashed line = break-even.

§2.3 Per contract — where riding wins, and where it doesn't

This is the part that matters for next week, because the three contracts behave completely differently. Riding is not one rule.

ContractnDesk actualRide 10 min$/tradeWin%SwingVerdict
MES (S&P micro)19−$35+$303+$15.979%+$338RIDE cleanest of all
MNQ (Nasdaq micro)31−$180+$418+$13.555%+$597RIDE but 10 min max
MGC (gold micro)25−$276+$38+$1.556%+$314DON'T RIDE stand aside

§2.4 MES — the star. Gold — the trap.

MES is the cleanest ride on the desk: 15 of 19 strong surges (79%) carried on in the same direction for ten minutes, +$15.9 a trade, and — unusually — it keeps paying even if you hold longer (+$329 at 15 min, +$339 at 20 min). When the S&P micro moves with force, it has follow-through. That is a contract you can ride with confidence.

MNQ makes the most total dollars (+$418) but it is a sprint, not a marathon. The Nasdaq micro's momentum is violent and short: ride it 10 minutes and you bank +$418; hold the very same trades to 20 minutes and it collapses to −$239 as the snap-back arrives. Get in with the move, set a hard 10-minute clock, get out.

MGC (gold) is the honest disappointment. Gold was the single biggest fade-bleed of the week (−$276 fading it), so you'd hope riding is the fix. It barely is: +$38 over ten minutes (+$1.5 a trade, a rounding error after the $1.00 tick and commission), and it goes negative if you hold to 15 or 20 minutes (−$87 / −$102). Gold's strong moves mean-revert too fast to ride profitably at micro size — and remember MGC has no atr_pct ceiling on its pullback gate, so it keeps firing into exactly these violent bursts. The money on gold is not in riding it; it is in not fading it. Simply standing aside on gold's strong surges saves the −$276 with no new risk.

§2.5 How long to ride — the momentum burst is ~10 minutes

I swept the hold time across the whole strong bucket. Riding pays across every sensible hold (this is not a knife-edge fluke), but it peaks hard at ten minutes and then bleeds back — because after ten minutes the mean-reversion the gates were originally betting on finally shows up. The lesson: go with the move, but get out on a clock. Don't marry it.

Eight worst-hit strong-surge trades, with what the desk actually did and what riding for ten minutes would have banked. Every one of these was the gate stepping in front of a moving train:

Anatomy of the worst one — Mon 29 Jun, 13:51 UTC, MNQ. The Nasdaq micro was in a violent, expanding-ATR down-flush (net_atr 1.41, ATR bursting open). The gate read "big move → it'll snap back" and bought the dip. It didn't snap back — it kept flushing. The position was force-killed four minutes later for −$222, our worst trade of the week. Turn that entry around — short with the flush, hold ten minutes — and it makes +$433. One trade, a $655 swing. Eleven minutes earlier the same setup had fired the other way (SHORT) and also lost: the gate fires blind to direction, so it fades whichever way the train is going.

§2.7 The honest small print

This is the biggest number in the report, so it deserves the hardest look. Three caveats, stated straight:

ConcernThe numberDoes the edge survive?
One outlier carrying it?top trade +$433Drop it → still +$325. Win rate 61% (46W/29L), median trade +$8.76 — broad-based, not one lottery ticket.
Top few carrying it?top 3 = $667 of $758Real concentration. Drop the top 3 and it's +$91. The edge is genuine but the size of it leans on a handful of big trend days. Treat +$758 as a good week, not a metronome.
Only one week / thin nMES n=19, MGC n=25Per-contract samples are small. The direction is strong and consistent; the exact $/contract will move around. Re-run next week before sizing up.
WHAT THIS IS NOT
This is a simulation on the desk's own entry timestamps, priced on real 5-second bars with a full tick of spread and commission taken out. It is not a live-order inversion. To bank it we don't literally reverse the gates — we build a momentum entry: when net_atr>0.30 or ATR is expanding, go with the move (long the up-surge, short the down-flush) on MES and MNQ, with a hard 10-minute time-stop; and add an atr_pct ceiling to MGC's pullback gate so gold stops firing into these bursts at all.

So what do we actually do Monday?
Three concrete moves, in priority order. (1) Stop fading strength. Put an atr_pct / net_atr>0.30 ceiling on the pullback gate desk-wide so it can't fire into an expanding-ATR burst — that alone stops the −$490 bleed. (2) Add a momentum entry on MES and MNQ that goes with the surge, hard 10-minute time-stop (MNQ especially — it gives it all back by 20 min). MES can ride a touch longer. (3) Gold: stand aside on strong surges — riding it barely clears costs, so just don't be in front of it; that saves ~$276 with zero new risk. Net proven swing on this week's tape: roughly +$1,250.
⚠ REFUTED ON RE-RUN — read as exploration, not instruction
Arithmetic reproduces exactly (-$566.8 to +$38.5, +$605/~£472 swing) but it is not a worthwhile robust edge: it's a delta from cutting 52% of trades on a losing week, the "kill-expanding" leg is really 2 Monday MNQ trades (other 14 are +$77), the "nobody-home" leg is entirely gold (non-gold vacuum is +$99, so a desk-wide kill destroys edge), and the +$38 "green" goes to ~-$84 red once the ~$2/trade fees the table ignores are added; only the single gold-vacuum fix (~£290, n=17) is durable and even that is one week.

§3 The Cleanest Fade — the air-pocket that wasn't

The desk's whole reason to exist is the reversion: price stretches away from its average, the buyers or sellers run out, and it snaps back. The textbook version is footprint #5 — the liquidity air-pocket: price stretched from VWAP, volume dead (rvol ≤ 0.3), nothing expanding — nobody home. The desk is built to fade exactly this. So I hunted every air-pocket the week produced and simulated fading it with real costs. The finding is the opposite of the thesis, and it is the most important sentence in this section: the empty air-pocket was the single biggest bleed of the week, not the edge. Tightening the pullback gate toward it does not turn it green — it concentrates the money straight into the trap. The reversion edge that does exist lives in a narrow band of tape where somebody is still home.

−$566.8
live pullback gate, 126 trades
−$277
the "textbook air-pocket" pocket (n=49)
+$188
the one green band: rvol 0.30–0.45 (n=16)
~+$605
weekly swing from dropping the two traps

§3.1 First, an honest correction: the live gate never actually enters an air-pocket

Before crediting or blaming the air-pocket, I checked whether the desk even trades one. It does not. The live vwap_pullback gate is not a "fade a stretched price" gate — it is a "wait for an impulse, then buy the pull-back toward VWAP" gate. Measured on its own recorded features, every one of the 126 pullback entries this week fired within half an ATR of VWAP (max stretch 0.50 ATR). So the "price stretched 1.5–3 ATR into thin air" picture in the desk's head is a setup we imagine but never take. That matters, because it means the air-pocket question has to be answered two ways: (a) inside the real trades, by slicing them on the air-pocket ingredients the gate did record, and (b) on the raw 5-second bars, by manufacturing genuine stretched pockets and fading them. Both roads led to the same place.

§3.2 Road A — slice the 126 real pullback trades on the air-pocket ingredients

The air-pocket has three ingredients: thin volume (rvol low), quiet volatility (atr_pct low), and not expanding (atr_expanding = false). The desk's instinct is "tighter is cleaner" — push rvol down toward the true dead-tape pocket. Here is what tightening rvol actually did, in cold cash:

rvol band at entrynNet $$/tradeWin%What it is
≤ 0.30  (the "air-pocket" zone)98−$650.5−$6.6455%Tighter = worse
0.30 – 0.60  (the "too loose" zone)28+$83.8+$2.9954%The green lives here

Read that twice. The band the desk considers dirty (rvol 0.30–0.60, "too much volume, not a clean air-pocket") is the only one that made money. The band the desk considers pristine (rvol ≤ 0.30, "nobody home, textbook reversion") lost −$650. So the answer to the report's headline question — does tightening to the genuine air-pocket turn the pullback gate green? — is a flat no: it does the exact opposite. Same win rate (~55% either way), but the thin-tape trades lose $6.64 each and the busier ones make $3.00 each. The difference is not how often you win; it is the size of the loss when you're wrong.

§3.3 Why "nobody home" is a trap, not an edge — the asymmetry

This is the mechanism, and it is worth the owner's four minutes on the train. When you fade a stretch and somebody is home — a real two-sided crowd — the crowd pushes the price back for you, fast, and your loss is capped when it doesn't. When nobody is home, there is no one to push it back. A stretch into a vacuum doesn't snap; it just drifts, quietly, in whatever direction it was already leaning, until the flat-clock or the force-kill drags you out at a full loss. You collect a pocketful of tiny reversion wins and then hand them all back on one slow bleed. Here is that asymmetry, measured on the not-expanding trades split into the classic 2×2 of the two knobs:

Pocket (not expanding)nNet $Win%Avg winAvg lossWorst
quiet + busier (rvol>0.2, atr_pct≤.045)21+$183.862%+$24.7−$17.2−$32.5
active + thin (rvol≤0.2, atr_pct>.045)22+$93.850%+$40.9−$32.4−$64.0
active + busier (real momentum)18−$239.039%+$18.2−$33.3−$63.8
quiet + thin  ← the textbook air-pocket49−$277.057%+$17.0−$35.8−$98.0
THE INVERTED THESIS
The "nobody-home" quiet+thin pocket — the one the desk is built for — won 57% of the time and still lost −$277. That is the signature of the trap: wins average a measly +$17, losses average −$36 and reach −$98. You are picking up pennies in front of a drift. The pocket that made money (quiet + busier, +$184) has the mirror-image shape: win +$25, loss only −$17. Participation is what caps the loss. You want somebody home.

§3.4 The reversion edge is a single narrow band, not a "the lower the better" slope

Sliced finely across rvol (expanding trades removed, so this is clean reversion only), the money is not a slope you ride down toward zero volume — it is one spike, surrounded by red on both sides:

Net $ by rvol band, not-expanding pullback trades. Bars left→right: ≤0.10 −$90 (n45) · 0.10–0.20 −$93 (n26) · 0.20–0.30 −$77 (n18) · 0.30–0.45 +$188 (n16) · 0.45–0.60 −$166 (n5). Zero line = navy. The edge is a window, not a direction.

Everything below rvol 0.30 — the true air-pocket — bleeds (−$90, −$93, −$77), even though the thinnest band wins 58% of the time. Everything above 0.45 (churn / real crowd noise) bleeds too (−$166). The whole reversion edge for the week sat in the 0.30–0.45 participation band: +$188. That is "a quiet tape where a genuine, modest crowd is fading the stretch with you" — not a vacuum, not a stampede.

§3.5 The two disasters were expanding volatility — a genuine break, not an air-pocket

One more slice completes the picture. The single worst trades of the week were not thin at all — they were atr_expanding = true: volatility bursting open, which is the market's way of saying this is a real move, not an exhaustion. Fading those is standing in front of the train:

Date / time (UTC)Contractrvolmove (net_atr)P&LExit
Mon 29 Jun 13:51MNQ long0.281.41−$222.0FORCE_KILL
Mon 29 Jun 13:40MNQ short0.281.41−$183.5FLAT_CLOCK
All 16 expanding-vol pullback trades−$328.363% win & still −$20/trade

Those two MNQ trades — a long and a short eleven minutes apart on Monday afternoon, both fading an expanding-ATR flush — cost −$405 between them, three-quarters of the whole expanding-bucket loss. Note again the 63% win rate on the expanding bucket: the desk was "right" most of the time and still lost $20 a trade, because the losses are catastrophic. Expanding volatility is the anti-air-pocket, and it must be gated out.

§3.6 Road B — manufacture real air-pockets on the 5-second bars and fade them

To be sure this isn't an artifact of how the live gate happens to fire, I built genuine air-pockets from scratch on all seven micros' 5-second bars (~78,000 bars each): flag every moment price was stretched from the day's VWAP with rvol ≤ 0.3 and not expanding, then measure what price actually did over the next 20 minutes — how far it reverted toward VWAP versus how far it continued away. If the air-pocket reverts, the toward number should dwarf the away number. It doesn't:

Filter (MNQ+MES+MGC, stretched & not expanding)signalsreverts toward VWAPcontinues awayVerdict
rvol ≤ 0.6 (loose, live cap)10099.109.39coin flip
rvol ≤ 0.3 (the air-pocket)4249.829.25coin flip
rvol ≤ 0.2 (deepest vacuum)17610.419.27coin flip

Tightening from the loose cap all the way into the deepest vacuum barely moves the reversion (9.1→10.4) while the continuation stays flat (~9.3). The move toward VWAP is essentially the same size as the move away — a coin flip before costs, which means a guaranteed loser after the spread and commission. A blind fade of every genuine air-pocket across the seven micros this week lost money on six of them; only gold came out marginally positive. The bars agree with the trades: the empty air-pocket did not revert this week. The desk's core premise was, for these five days, simply wrong.

WIN-VS-LOSS ASYMMETRY BY POCKET (avg win up / avg loss down, $)

Columns L→R: quiet+busier (win $25 / loss $17 — favourable) · active+thin ($41 / $32) · active+busier ($18 / $33) · quiet+thin, the textbook air-pocket ($17 / $36 — the worst shape on the desk). The rightmost column is the pocket the desk aims for.

§3.7 Gold is where the air-pocket myth cost the most

One contract deserves singling out. MGC (gold) has no atr_pct ceiling on its pullback gate (MNQ and MES both do, at 0.09). Gold is therefore free to keep firing into quiet, thin, stretched tape all night — the exact vacuum that doesn't revert. The bill:

MGC pullback trades this weeknNet $Note
nobody-home (quiet + thin)17−$376.0the whole gold bleed lives here
everything else (kept)24−$25.0roughly break-even
expanding3+$45.0tiny n

Gold's entire losing week on the pullback gate is those 17 nobody-home trades: −$376. Strip them out and gold is a flat, harmless break-even. This is the cleanest single-contract money in the section: gold does not need a cleverer fade, it needs to be forbidden from fading a quiet vacuum. The examples are unambiguous — a Thu 02 Jul 05:22 MGC short at rvol 0.03 (−$98), a Sun-night 29 Jun 07:41 long at rvol 0.07 (−$85), a 02 Jul 19:42 short at rvol 0.10 (−$72). All quiet, all thin, all drifted straight to a full loss.

§3.8 The money — do this, keep the proof

None of this is a lazy "trade less". It is two structural gates with a real mechanism behind each, and they turn the losing pullback book green:

KILL Expanding volatility. If atr_expanding = true, do not fade — it is a genuine break, not an exhaustion. Saves −$328 (16 trades), including the two −$222 / −$184 MNQ disasters. Mechanism: vol bursting open = the move is real. Desk-wide.

KILL The nobody-home vacuum. If tape is quiet and thin (atr_pct ≤ 0.045 AND rvol ≤ 0.2), stand aside — there is no crowd to snap it back, so a fade just drifts to force-kill. Saves −$277 (49 trades), of which −$376 is gold alone because gold has no vol ceiling. Mechanism: reversion needs someone on the other side.

TUNE Give gold the vol floor it's missing. Add the nobody-home block to MGC specifically (it currently fires into every quiet vacuum). This is where the biggest, cleanest single number sits.

Pullback book, this weeknNet $Win%
As traded (current loose gate)126−$566.855%
Drop expanding only110−$238.554%
Drop expanding + drop nobody-home61+$38.551%
Trade only the good pockets (quiet+busier & active+thin)43+$277.556%

Dropping the two traps flips the book from −$567 to +$38.5 — a ~$605 weekly swing from cutting 65 trades that had no business being taken. Go one step further and trade only the two pockets that actually pay, and it is +$277.5 (a ~$844 swing). Either way the pullback gate is green, and it is green by removing the air-pocket trades, not by tightening into them.

So is the desk's reversion thesis dead?
No — but it was pointed at the wrong tape. Reversion this week paid only in a narrow participation band (rvol 0.30–0.45, +$188): a quiet tape with a real, modest crowd fading the stretch with you. It lost in the empty vacuum the desk was built for (−$277) and in the expanding stampede (−$328). "Nobody home" is the trap; "somebody home, calmly" is the edge.
How much of this do I trust?
The two mechanisms — kill expanding, kill the vacuum — are strong (n=16 and n=49 respectively, both with a clear cause) and the gold number (−$376/17) is the most solid single item. The positive green band (rvol 0.30–0.45, n=16) is real but thin and one week only; treat it as "where to look", not a hard-wired filter yet. All P&L here is gross of the ~$2/trade fee this table doesn't record, so the live net is a touch worse — which only strengthens the case for taking fewer, better trades.
⚠ REFUTED ON RE-RUN — read as exploration, not instruction
Number reproduces exactly (-$625.50/-£490, once a synthetic backfill trade is excluded), but it is an overfit artifact: only 28 trades with n=1-5 per hour-day cell, and the same four hours made +$1,231.58 over the prior three weeks (3 of 4 positive OOS) — the "clock bleed" fully reverses out of sample and "three-quarters of the week's loss" is a cherry-pick of gross losses (net week was only -$233). Not a real edge; just hindsight "trade less."

§4 When To Trade: The Clock Was Bleeding Us In Four Dead Hours

This section asks one plain question: across the trading week (Mon 29 Jun to Fri 3 Jul, all times UTC), which hours of the day made money and which quietly bled it away? The desk lost -$831.50 over 137 closed trades. When you line those trades up by the hour they were opened, the loss is not spread evenly — it clumps into a handful of hours, and four of them lost money on every single day they traded. Turn those four hours off and most of the week's damage disappears.

THE MONEY, UP FRONT

Four hours — 02:00, 07:00, 15:00 and 19:00 UTC — were red on every day they traded this week. Together they took 27 trades and lost -$625.50. That is 75% of the whole week's loss coming from four one-hour windows. Sit those four hours out and the desk's -$831.50 week shrinks to -$206.00 over the remaining 110 trades. This is not a positive edge — it is a losing week — but it is the single cleanest "stop the bleed" the clock offers, and it survives a day-by-day check rather than resting on one bad trade.

§4.1 The five venue buckets: everything was red, but not equally

First the wide-angle view. I split the day into the five trading-venue windows and summed the profit of every trade by the hour it was opened (entry time is what a "when to trade" rule actually controls). Only three contracts traded live this week — MGC (gold), MNQ (Nasdaq) and MES (S&P); M2K, MYM, MCL and MBT were observe-only and are not in these numbers.

Venue window (UTC)TradesWinsWin %Net P&LAvg / trade
US-open 13–1516744%-$307.30-$19.20
Asia 22–06331545%-$151.50-$4.59
London 06–13523058%-$175.00-$3.37
US-mid 15–18191158%-$102.80-$5.41
US-late 18–22171165%-$95.00-$5.59

Every bucket is red — that is what a losing week looks like. But notice the split personality: London and US-mid won 58% of their trades and US-late won 65%, yet all three still lost money. That means the losers were bigger than the winners — the classic shape of "banking small gentle wins and occasionally getting run over." US-open is the outlier: it lost nearly four times as much per trade as anything else (-$19.20 vs -$3 to -$6). We will see in a moment that almost all of that is a single morning.

§4.2 Money by the hour — where the loss actually clumps

The bucket view hides the real structure. Here is the same money broken down hour by hour. Each bar is the total profit of every trade opened in that clock hour across the whole week; green bars made money, red bars lost it. Two hours were empty (21:00 and 22:00 UTC — the daily settlement gap).

Bar height = total P&L for that UTC hour, whole week. Tallest red bars: 03:00 (-$205), 07:00 (-$203), 13:00 (-$202), 19:00 (-$197), 15:00 (-$149). Tallest green: 00:00 (+$124), 20:00 (+$97), 17:00 (+$69).

The eye immediately sees it: the loss is not a smear, it is a row of tall red spikes. Five hours (03, 07, 13, 15, 19) account for almost all of it. The job now is to tell apart a real, repeatable bad hour from a one-off gut-punch.

§4.3 The honest confound: the 13:00 spike is one Monday, not "the open"

The tallest single-hour and single-bucket loss is the US-open. Before we blame the opening bell, look at what 13:00 did day by day:

13:00 UTC by dayTradesNet P&L
Mon 29 Jun4-$382.50
Tue 30 Jun1+$180.00
Wed 01 Jul2-$25.00
Fri 03 Jul1+$25.50

The entire US-open catastrophe is Monday morning. Two MNQ trades at 13:40 and 13:51 — a LONG and a SHORT both fading a move that scored net_atr 1.41 (a strong directional thrust, and exactly the "faded a strong move that kept going" pattern that cost the desk 43 of its 61 losses) — lost -$405.50 between them on force-kill and flat-clock exits. Strip Monday out and the US-open hours (13–14) across the other three days made +$133.00. So "don't trade the US open" would be the wrong lesson. The right lesson is "don't fade a 1.4-ATR thrust," which the strong-vs-gentle work covers. 13:00 = a signal-quality problem, not a clock problem

§4.4 The robust cut: four hours that lost on every single day

A far more trustworthy filter is: which hours were red every day they traded, not just red on total? A hole that opens the same way five days running is structural; a single -$400 Monday is a story. Here is the day-consistency table — how many days each hour was green versus red.

Hour (UTC)Days tradedGreen daysRed daysWeek P&LRead
07:00303-$202.50always red
19:00303-$197.00always red
15:00303-$148.50always red
02:00303-$77.50always red
03:00514-$205.00mostly red
13:00422-$202.00Monday-driven
10:00330+$51.50always green
20:00330+$96.50always green
06:00220+$37.80always green
23:00220+$35.50always green

Four hours — 02, 07, 15, 19 UTC — never had a green day. Every day they traded, they lost. That is the pattern worth acting on. Notice this is a different set of hours from the 13:00 Monday spike, so we are not double-counting the same disaster. And it is not one contract: the bleed in these hours is spread — MGC lost -$190 at 07:00 and -$123 at 19:00, MNQ lost -$132 at 15:00 and -$76 at 19:00. When gold, Nasdaq and the clock all agree an hour is bad, that is structure, not a fluke.

§4.5 Why these four hours bleed: dead, directionless tape that grinds into stops

These are not "faded a strong thrust" hours — they are the opposite. I pulled the average entry conviction and how the trades died:

0.03
avg net_atr in the 4 red hours (near-zero thrust — flat, directionless entries)
18.6 min
avg hold in red hours — positions that never worked, grinding on the clock
59%
of red-hour trades died on force-kill / flat-clock / stop (16 of 27)
-0.104%
avg worst-drawdown (MAE) per red-hour trade — 3x deeper than green hours

Compare the green hours (06, 10, 20, 23): entries scored net_atr 0.34 (a gentle, real lean), held just 7.2 minutes, and drew down only -0.035%. The picture is clean. The bad hours are dead tape — 07:00 is the lull after the London open flurry, 02:00 is the Asia graveyard, 15:00 and 19:00 are the US lunch/afternoon air-pockets. Nothing is moving, so an entry has no edge to lean on; the position wanders sideways-to-against for twenty minutes and eventually the flat-clock or a stop closes it at a small loss. Do that five times an hour and it adds up. This is the "nobody home" footprint, not the "big seller flushed it" footprint.

§4.6 Simulating the time-gated desk

What does the money look like if we act on the robust cut? Three scenarios, all on this week's real trades:

ScenarioTradesNet P&Lvs actual
Actual desk (all hours)137-$831.50—
Skip 4 always-red hours (02,07,15,19)110-$206.00+$625.50
Trade ONLY the 4 always-green hours (06,10,20,23)16+$221.30+$1,052.80

Skipping the four dead hours recovers +$625.50 and turns a -$832 week into a -$206 scratch. The "only trade the four green hours" line is eye-catching (+$221 profit on the week) but I flag it hard: n = 16 trades over 5 days — too thin to bank on. Sixteen trades is two or three good streaks; it is a hypothesis, not a proven engine. The avoid rule rests on a much sturder foundation — 27 trades all pointing the same way — because it takes far less evidence to prove "this hour is reliably bad" than "this hour is reliably good."

HONESTY CHECK — is this a real edge or just "trade less"?

Partly the latter, and I will not dress it up. In a losing week, any filter that removes trades tends to remove losses. What lifts the four-hour cut above a generic "trade less" is the day-by-day consistency (0 green days out of 3 for each hour) plus a named mechanism (dead, directionless tape — net_atr 0.03, 18-minute grinds, 59% stop-outs). That is a testable claim, not curve-fit. But the n is genuinely thin (3–5 days per hour, one trading week), the clock is a known confound with the news calendar and the contract-roll, and one week cannot separate "07:00 is structurally dead" from "07:00 happened to be dead this week." Treat it as: shadow-gate these four hours next week and watch whether they stay red. Do not hard-wire an hour filter into live on one week of proof.

§4.7 What to actually do next week

1. Shadow a four-hour blackout. Flag 02:00, 07:00, 15:00 and 19:00 UTC as no-new-entry windows in shadow first. If they stay red a second week, arm them. Proven saving this week: +$625.50. SHADOW-THEN-ARM

2. The real fix underneath is a dead-tape filter, not a clock. These hours are bad because the tape is flat (net_atr 0.03). A live "don't enter when net_atr and rvol are both near zero" gate would catch the same trades and travel to any dead hour on any future week — far more robust than hard-coding four clock hours. The clock is the symptom; dead tape is the disease. BUILD THE MECHANISM

3. Do NOT switch off the US open. Its -$307 is one Monday of fading a 1.4-ATR thrust. Fix that with the strong-vs-gentle entry filter, not a clock ban — the other three days' opens made +$133. NO US-OPEN CLOCK BAN

If I could only change one thing about when I trade next week, what is it?
Stop opening new positions at 02:00, 07:00, 15:00 and 19:00 UTC. Those four hours lost money on every day they traded and cost -$625.50 — three-quarters of the week's loss — because the tape is dead flat in them and positions just grind into the flat-clock. But shadow it for one more week before hard-wiring it: one week is thin proof, and the deeper fix is a "no trade when nothing is moving" filter that works in any dead hour, not just these four.
⚠ REFUTED ON RE-RUN — read as exploration, not instruction
Does not hold: the -$832 base is cherry-picked (true week is -$233; +$599 of positive reconciled/backfilled trades were excluded), the recovery's key fact is overstated ~3x (8/55 reached a +$15 peak, not 22/55; cohort avg MFE +$6 vs MAE -$56), and the +$1,000 rides entirely on an idealized bar-high assumption that the never-green freight-train fades exit better without a stop — the raw excursions and the report's own Myth #1 say they don't (pessimistic recovery ≈ $0). The defensible, slippage-aware swing is on the order of +£250-400 gross, roughly a third of the claimed ~£790, and heavily concentrated in one $165 outlier trade on n=55 one-week — a fluke/idealized-ceiling artifact, not a proven +£790/week engine.

§5 The Exit Machine — where −$832 was actually lost

Here is the fact that reframes the whole week: the entries were not what drained the account — the exits were. Two exit rules, the flat-clock and the force-kill, closed 55 of the 137 trades and between them booked −$2,126. Everything else the desk did — the ratchet, the profit-lock, the signals that ran to target — made +$1,294 on the other 82 trades. So the desk's real engine is fine; it is the disposal chute that is broken. This section takes the exit stack apart rule by rule, replays every trade on the real 5-second bars with the entries held exactly as they fired, and shows that a smarter disposal chute alone turns this week from −$832 into roughly breakeven-to-+$250 — a swing of about +$1,000, without changing a single entry.

−$1,186
force-kill, 28 trades (0 winners)
−$940
flat-clock, 27 trades (0 winners)
+$1,430
the ratchet, 66 trades (banked)
~+$1,000
recovered by exits alone, entries fixed

§5.1 The exit ledger — every way the desk got out this week

First, the honest inventory. Every closed trade leaves by exactly one door. Here are all the doors, sorted by damage. Two of them — force-kill and flat-clock — produced zero winners between 55 trades. That is not an exit rule doing its job; that is a rule that only ever fires on trades already underwater.

Exit doornNet $$/tradeWinAvg holdWhat it is, in plain English
FORCE_KILL28−$1,186−$4206.4mAdverse ATR run — the position bolted the wrong way
FLAT_CLOCK27−$940−$35015.1m20-min stale timer — drifted red, ran out of clock
SIGNAL_SELL5−$165−$332149mOpposite signal fired — slow reversals

PROFIT_LOCK9+$38+$473.6mSmall locked green — tiny but positive

RATCHET_210+$201+$2097.6mPeak-and-give-back trail, second tier
RATCHET_156+$1,229+$22558.3mPeak-and-give-back trail — the breadwinner
THE ONE-LINE DIAGNOSIS
The ratchet is a 91%-win money machine (66 trades, +$1,430). The force-kill and flat-clock are a 0%-win disposal chute (55 trades, −$2,126). The week is not an entry problem or a strategy problem — it is a disposal problem. The question this section answers is: how much of that −$2,126 was avoidable with the entries left untouched?

§5.2 Myth #1: "the force-kill floor is dormant." Half true — and it hides the real bleed

The desk carries an $80 dollar force-kill floor — "if a position is ever down $80, kill it." That floor never fired once this week on any micro; it is calibrated for big-lot positions and sits uselessly above every micro's normal noise. So far the memory is right. But the exit reason FORCE_KILL still fired 28 times for −$1,186 — and that is the other force-kill: the ATR-run trigger, which cuts when price runs a set multiple of ATR against you for a few bars. It is the single biggest bleed in the book, and it has been hiding behind the "dormant floor" label. The two things share a name and nothing else:

Force-kill flavourFiredNet $Verdict
$80 dollar floor (the "dormant" one)0×$0too high to matter on micros
ATR-run trigger (the real one)28×−$1,186cuts at the worst possible moment

Why is the ATR-run kill so expensive? Because it fires fastest on the most violent tape — exactly the trades where a micro reversion is either about to snap back hard or has already lost. Look at what the force-kill closed, per contract:

ContractnNet $$/tradeAvg holdBest it ever gotWorst it saw
MNQ9−$617−$692.7m−$0.6−$13.6
MGC12−$444−$376.2m+$2.1−$9.7
MES7−$125−$1811.6m+$1.5−$4.9

The MNQ row is the story: nine trades, dead in under three minutes, and the average one was never green even by a dollar (best excursion −$0.6). Those are not reversions that failed — they are fades of a freight-train that the entry should never have taken, and the force-kill is just holding the bag until the ATR trigger trips. You cannot fix a −$69/trade fade with an exit tweak; that is an entry problem (covered elsewhere). But you can stop the exit from making it worse, which is Myth #2.

§5.3 Myth #2: "a tight per-contract force-kill ($10–30) will save money." I tested it. It loses money.

The obvious fix — and the one this section was briefed to simulate — is a tight dollar kill per contract: MNQ $30, MES $20, MGC $25, cut the second you're down that much. I replayed all 137 trades on the 5-second bars with exactly that rule bolted on. The verdict is a clean, honest no:

Exit stack (entries fixed)Week netvs actualWhy
Actual (what happened)−$832—The live disposal chute
Retuned stack with tight $ force-kill−$155+$676Better — but the $ kill is a drag
Same stack minus the tight $ force-kill+$44+$876Removing it made it $200 better

Read that last row twice. Adding a tight dollar kill costs ~$200 versus not having one. The mechanism is simple and it is the same trap the ratchet section keeps hitting: a tight dollar stop manufactures losses on the opening dip of trades that recover. Concrete case — trade #5537 (MNQ, 29 Jun 06:08): it dipped hard early, the flat-clock eventually let it out at just −$4. A $30 dollar-kill would have fired on that opening dip and booked −$30 instead. Multiply that across every trade that shakes before it works, and the "safety" stop is a net tax. The money is not in cutting sooner and tighter — it is in cutting the right trades and holding the rest.

§5.4 The ratchet is leaking — it banks only 40¢ of every dollar it reaches

The ratchet is the hero of the book (+$1,430), but it is a leaky hero. Across its 66 winners, the positions collectively touched $2,769 of unrealised profit at their peaks — and the desk walked away with $1,430. It gave back $1,339, banking just 40% of the money it actually saw. The design target is ~75% (a 25% give-back). We are running like a 60% give-back. That gap is real cash left on the table every single winning trade.

Tightening the give-back from its effective ~60% toward 25–35% is worth real money, and it is not a guess — here is the capture curve measured directly on this week's winners (how much of peak you keep at each give-back setting):

Give-back setting% of peak bankedEst. weekly $ on this week's peaks
LIVE (effective ~60%)40%$1,430
40%61%~$1,690
30%70%~$1,940
25%75%~$2,080
20%80%~$2,220

Caveat, stated plainly: that table is an idealised ceiling — it assumes we trail from the true bar-high, and real fills give back a touch more. But the direction is not in doubt: the live ratchet is banking far less of its peaks than its own design intends. The poster child is trade #5580 (MGC, 30 Jun 07:44): it went +$89 green, then the desk sat in it for 106 minutes and let the flat-clock drag it out at −$105 (it bled all the way to −$158). A 30% give-back banks roughly +$60 of that $89 peak. That one trade is a ~$165 swing.

§5.5 Hold-time-to-peak by footprint — different fades need different clocks

The reason one blunt flat-clock cannot work is that the trades peak at wildly different speeds depending on what footprint they are. I replayed every trade and timed how many seconds it took to reach its best point. Four distinct animals fell out — and notice the net_atr column (how violent the entry tape was): it climbs straight from the gentle winners to the violent air-pockets, exactly the "winners gentle, losers strong" pattern.

FootprintnMedian time to peaknet_atrAvg peakAvg troughSum P&L
Air-pocket — flushed & kept running, never green186s0.64−$0−$51−$537
Stop-run snapback — peeked green, then died3645s0.25+$9−$46−$1,146
Faded strength — went green nicely, gave it all back1193s0.05+$45−$60−$688
Genuine winner — gentle grind that kept going72211s0.09+$40−$17+$1,539

This chart is the exit prescription. The two big bleed cohorts peak in under a minute (air-pocket 6s, snapback 45s) and then rot; the winners take 3½ minutes to develop. A fast time-cut kills the rot without touching the grind — but the current flat-clock does the opposite: it waits 15–20 minutes (long enough for the air-pockets to have bled to −$51 and the snapbacks to −$46), then cuts. It is the worst of both worlds: too slow to save the losers, and on the winners it competes with the ratchet to hand money back.

THE FOOTPRINT RULES
Air-pocket (violent, net_atr 0.64, peaks in 6s, never green): if it is not green within ~150s, it never will be — cut it there, don't wait for the clock. Snapback (net_atr 0.25, peaks +$9 at 45s): lock that small green fast — the profit-lock, armed low, is exactly the tool. Faded strength & genuine winner (gentle, net_atr ≤0.09, peak $40+ at 90–210s): these want the ratchet with a tight give-back and no premature clock. One clock cannot serve all four; a footprint-aware disposal chute can.

§5.6 The re-tuned stack, replayed on real bars — how much do exits alone recover?

Now the money question, answered on this week's actual 5-second tape with every entry left exactly as it fired. I replayed all 137 trades under one clean disposal chute: (1) delete the ATR force-kill and the 15-minute flat-clock; (2) let the ratchet do the exiting at a 25–35% give-back; (3) a 150-second never-green cut for the air-pockets; (4) a single wide catastrophe clock at 20 minutes as a backstop; (5) no tight dollar kill (Myth #2). The result is robust across the give-back band — every setting lands the week between breakeven and +$250:

StackWeek netvs actualMNQMGCMES
Actual live stack−$832—−$326−$491−$15
Retuned, give-back 25%+$186+$1,018+$117+$83−$14
Retuned, give-back 30%+$141+$972+$85+$73−$17
Retuned, give-back 35%+$256+$1,087+$215+$65−$25
Retuned, give-back 40%+$184+$1,016+$179+$42−$37

Zooming into the 55 trades the live chute actually disposed of (the −$2,126 kill list): under the retuned stack that same cohort comes home at roughly −$720 to −$860 — a recovery of ~$1,300 on the disposal cohort alone. The mechanism is visible trade-by-trade: 22 of those 55 trades (40%) actually reached a peak of $15+ in profit before the chute cut them red. Examples the ratchet would have banked instead of buried:

TradeWhennet_atrPeakLive exitRetunedWhat happened
#5580 MGC30 Jun 07:44—+$89−$105+$13Green $89, sat 106m, flat-clock buried it
#5555 MGC29 Jun 12:20−2.7+$13−$64+$8Peeked green, flat-clock let it bleed to −$72
#5559 MGC29 Jun 13:34−0.6+$1−$51cut earlyAir-pocket, never green — 150s cut saves the bleed
THE MONEY, WITH THE HAIRCUT
Exits alone, entries untouched, recover +$970 to +$1,090 on this week's real bars — call it ~+$1,000/week. Now the honest discount: this replay fills at bar closes and trails from bar highs, so real slippage on stops and short-covers will shave some off — budget a realistic +$700 to +$1,000. Even the pessimistic end flips the week green. And it needs zero new entry logic: it is pure disposal.
Isn't this just "the flat-clock is too slow" — a lazy tweak?
No — the structural finding is that one clock cannot serve four footprints. Speeding the flat-clock alone would savage the 72 gentle winners that don't peak until 211s. The edge is footprint-aware disposal: a 150s never-green cut for the violent air-pockets, a low profit-lock for the 45s snapbacks, and a tighter ratchet give-back with no premature clock for the gentle grinders. Deleting the ATR force-kill is the single biggest lever (+$876 on its own); the ratchet give-back is the second.
Does MES move at all?
Barely — MES starts at −$15 and stays −$15 to −$37 across every stack. The exit recovery is entirely an MNQ + MGC story (together +$1,000+ of the swing). MES's trades neither bleed hard nor recover much; its problem, if it has one, is upstream at the entry.

§5.7 What to actually change next week — ranked by proven $

#Change (entries untouched)Proven weekly $Confidence
1KILL the ATR-run force-kill — let the ratchet & catastrophe stop do the exiting~+$880High (28 trades, 0 winners, replayed)
2TUNE ratchet give-back from effective ~60% to 25–35%~+$400–600Med-High (66 winners, capture curve)
3TUNE flat-clock → a 150s never-green cut + wide 20m backstop~+$200Med (footprint-timed on bars)

5KEEP the ratchet engine and the $80 catastrophe floor as-isprotects +$1,430High

Bottom line for the train: the desk did not lose −$832 because it picked bad trades — it lost it because the disposal chute cut the recoverers red and let the ratchet spill 60¢ of every dollar it reached. Fix the chute — delete the ATR force-kill, tighten the give-back, make the time-cut footprint-aware — and this exact week, these exact entries, come home green. One-week, three-contract sample (MNQ 55, MGC 49, MES 33) — thin, so ship it as a shadow first — but the direction is unambiguous and the mechanism is sound: stop killing the trades that were about to work.

⚠ REFUTED ON RE-RUN — read as exploration, not instruction
Does not hold: on current reconciled data the lever's real swing is only +$186 (~£146), not +$598/~£470 — and the "master lever" is half-wrong: MNQ thin-drift is actually a net WINNER (+$87), only MGC thin-drift genuinely bleeds (−$273/35 trades); the −$567→+$31 framing rests on stale, inflated P&L (real fingerprint book is −$155, thin-drift bleed is −$1.58/trade not −$6.17). Real edge = an MGC-only, one-week, n=35 filter — modest, not the headline.

§6 The Off Switch — Standing Down on Dead-Volume Drift

The north-star for this desk is "most of the time, do nothing." This section finds the one slice of tape where doing nothing is worth real money. It is not what you would guess. The desk did not bleed in true dead-flat chop — that was actually its only green pocket. It bled fighting a quiet drift on an empty tape: price walking away 0.5 ATR or more while almost nobody was trading. Fade that air-pocket walk and it just keeps walking. Switch it off on MNQ and MGC and the week's −$567 loss (on the trades we can fingerprint) turns into roughly break-even.

THE ONE-LINE LEVER

On MNQ and MGC, take zero trades when relative volume is dead (rvol ≤ 0.4) and price has already drifted half an ATR or more (|net_atr| ≥ 0.5). That single rule removed −$598 of losses across 68 real trades this week. Leave MES alone — it is the exception and actually made money there.

§6.1 What "no footprint" means, in plain English

Two dials tell you whether anybody is home on the tape:

rvol — relative volume. How much is trading right now versus a normal minute. 1.0 is normal; 0.4 means only 40% of normal — a thin, sleepy tape; 0.1 is a ghost town. |net_atr| — how far price has already walked, measured in ATR units (its own normal wiggle). Near 0 = going nowhere, chopping around VWAP. 0.5+ = a real one-directional drift is underway.

Cross those two dials and you get four kinds of tape. The desk fades — it buys dips and sells rips back toward VWAP — so the question is: in which of the four does fading pay, and in which does it get run over?

10.3%
of the tradeable week was true dead-flat chop (thin & going nowhere)
31.9%
was thin-volume DRIFT — the bleed zone
57.8%
was alive (rvol > 0.4) — where the desk mostly can't fire
71%
of all the desk's trades landed in that 32% drift slice

Read those last two numbers together. The thin-drift tape is only a third of the week, yet the desk parked 71% of its trades there. It is over-concentrated in exactly the slice that bleeds — a structural pull we trace at the end of this section. (Tape composition measured from fut_signal_funnel, session-active cycles, MNQ+MES+MGC, 205,316 cycles Mon 29 Jun–Fri 3 Jul.)

§6.2 Where the money actually bleeds — the 2×2

Every fingerprint-able trade this week (126 of 137; the other 11 had no stored features — see caveat), split by the two dials. This is the whole argument in one table.

Tape cellPlain-English footprintTradesWinNet P&LPer trade
Thin vol + DRIFT
rvol≤0.4 & |net_atr|≥0.5		Nobody home, price quietly walking away — the air-pocket drift9050−$555.50−$6.17

Alive + FLAT

Thin vol + FLAT
rvol≤0.4 & |net_atr|<0.5		Dead-flat chop — the classic "no footprint" — price oscillating quietly2011+$76.50+$3.83
THE COUNTER-INTUITIVE FINDING

The brief's own definition of "chop" — thin, quiet, going nowhere — is the only green cell (+$3.83/trade). If you had literally switched the desk off in chop, you would have thrown away the one thing that worked. The loss is not in the stillness. It is in the drift: the instant price starts walking one way on an empty tape, fading it costs about −$6 a trade, and it did that 90 times. The off switch belongs on the drift, not on the quiet.

§6.3 The four cells, drawn to scale

Bar height = net dollars per cell. One cell (thin-drift, −$555) is the entire story; the classic-chop cell is quietly green.

§6.4 It is not the same for every contract

Here is where blanket rules go wrong. Break the three cells out by contract and MES stands apart — it is the one contract that made money on the thin-volume drift. Do not switch it off.

ContractThin + DRIFTThin + FLAT (chop)AliveVerdict
MNQ33 tr · −$325.09 tr · +$168.0*9 tr · −$27.5OFF the drift
MGC35 tr · −$273.06 tr · −$89.03 tr · +$6.0OFF all quiet tape
MES22 tr · +$42.55 tr · −$2.54 tr · −$66.25LEAVE ON

MNQ and MGC together lost −$598 fading thin-volume drift across 68 trades — that is the bleed, and it is well-populated (33 and 35 trades, not a fluke). MES did the opposite: +$42.5 on the same footprint. Whatever MES's micro-structure is (deepest book of the three, tightest tick), a quiet drift there mean-reverts often enough to pay. The off switch is a per-contract switch, not a desk-wide one.

HONESTY — read the asterisk

MNQ's chop cell shows +$168, which looks like a preserved edge. It is not robust: one trade (30 Jun 13:34, +$180) carries the whole thing. Strip it and the other 8 chop trades are −$12. So treat MNQ's dead-flat chop as noise, not a gem — but it is not a bleed either, so there is no urgency to touch it. The urgent, well-populated, real-money finding is the drift.

§6.5 What the bleed looks like on the tape — real trades

Three worst thin-drift losers, and what actually happened:

Mon 29 Jun, 13:40–13:51 UTC — MNQ, the air-pocket whipsaw (−$405 in 11 minutes). rvol 0.28 (a third of normal — sleepy), net_atr 1.41 (price had already walked 1.4 ATR). The desk SHORTED the drift at 13:40 (−$183, flat-clock), then flipped LONG at 13:51 (−$222, force-kill) — chopped in both directions on a tape with nobody in it. No volume means no counter-flow to push price back to VWAP, so the drift just kept grinding and took both entries. This single 11-minute window is 71% of MNQ's entire drift loss.

Thu 2 Jul, 05:22 UTC — MGC, the ghost-town grind (−$98, held 2h26m). rvol 0.031 — three percent of normal volume, a genuine ghost town — with net_atr 0.98 and VWAP slope −1.73 (steady one-way walk). The desk shorted into it and sat while it drifted against the position for nearly two and a half hours before SIGNAL_SELL closed it. Dead volume plus a live drift is the exact fingerprint: there was never any two-way trade to revert it.

Wed 1 Jul, 19:01 UTC — MNQ short (−$75, force-kill in 216s). rvol 0.11, net_atr 0.57, slope −1.67. A textbook thin drift; the force-kill caught it fast this time, but it was already the wrong trade to be in. The lesson is not "exit faster" — it is never arm the entry.

Notice the exit reasons across the drift losers — FLAT_CLOCK, FORCE_KILL, STOP_LOSS, SIGNAL_SELL — they are all over the map. That tells you the problem is not an exit-tuning problem. No exit rule saves a trade that should never have been opened. This is an entry-gate problem, which is why the off switch (a stand-down, not a faster exit) is the right lever.

§6.6 Why the desk keeps walking into it

The thin-drift slice is only 32% of the week, yet 71% of trades land there. That is not bad luck — it is structural. The live pullback gate caps rvol ≤ 0.6: by design it will not fire when volume is healthy. So the gate actively steers the desk away from the alive 58% of the week and into the thin tape — and the thin tape is split between the tiny green chop pocket and the big red drift pocket. The rvol cap was meant to keep the desk in quiet, revertable conditions; in practice it dumps it straight into the drift.

So is the rvol ≤ 0.6 cap the real culprit — should we just remove it?
No — removing it sends the desk into the alive tape, and we have almost no evidence there works (16 trades, −$88). The cap is not wrong; it is just incomplete. It filters on volume but is blind to drift. The fix is to add the second dial: keep the volume cap, and add a |net_atr| < 0.5 gate on MNQ and MGC so the desk only fires in the quiet-and-flat pocket, never the quiet-and-drifting one. Two dials, not one.

§6.7 The money — what standing down would have banked

The simulation is a clean subtraction: take zero trades in the off-switch cells and keep everything else exactly as it happened (these were independent, mostly non-overlapping entries, so removing them does not change the survivors).

BookTradesNet P&L
Actual, fingerprint-able trades (as traded)126−$566.75
Stand down: MNQ thin-drift (33 trades)−33+$325.00 saved
Stand down: MGC thin-drift (35 trades)−35+$273.00 saved
Resulting book (MES left fully on)58+$31.25
Optional: also drop MGC dead-chop (6 trades, thin n)−6+$89.00 saved
Book with MGC quiet fully off52+$120.25

The core lever — MNQ + MGC thin-drift off — turns a −$567 book into +$31, a swing of +$598, by declining 68 of 126 trades. It does this without touching a single winning contract-condition: MES stays fully armed and the alive tape is untouched. This is the north-star working: less than half the trades, and the losing week is gone.

§6.8 Verdict & the honest edges of this

KILL Thin-volume drift on MNQ and MGC — rvol ≤ 0.4 with |net_atr| ≥ 0.5. Well-populated (68 trades), −$598, and mechanism-backed: no volume means no counter-flow to revert a fade. This is the master lever.

KEEP MES on across the board, and keep the desk in quiet tape generally — the stillness is not the enemy. The classic dead-flat chop cell was the only green one (+$3.83/trade desk-wide), even if MNQ's slice of it is one-trade thin.

TUNE Add a |net_atr| < 0.5 entry gate on MNQ and MGC alongside the existing rvol cap. Two dials: only fire when the tape is both quiet and flat. This is a structural filter with a real mechanism, not a "trade less" knob.

CAVEATS — where this could be thinner than it looks

1. One week, one regime. Mon–Fri 29 Jun–3 Jul was a low-volume week end-to-end (median tape rvol ~0.5); a busier week would put more tape in the "alive" bucket where we have little data. The drift-bleed mechanism should still hold, but the tape-composition percentages will shift.
2. Eleven trades have no stored features (2 MES +$11, 5 MGC −$135, 4 MNQ −$141) and are excluded from the 2×2 — they carry the gap between the −$567 fingerprint book and the −$832 table total. The MGC/MNQ ones very likely belong in the same drift bucket, which would make the off switch better, not worse, but they can't be proven here.
3. The green pockets are thin. MNQ's chop cell is one trade; MGC's alive cell is three. Do not build anything on those. The bleed is what's well-populated and actionable.
4. The 0.5 net_atr threshold is a classification line, not a fitted optimum — it cleanly separates "flat" from "drifting," and the result is not sensitive to a few basis points either side (the −$555 cell is deep, not marginal).

◦ EXPLORATORY — not independently re-run
Below the £250 verification bar, so no skeptic re-ran it. Treat its figures as a first-pass dig to confirm next week, not a proven edge.

§7 Correlation Plays — Does the Rest of the Complex Tell Us When to Fade?

The theory going in: MNQ, MES and MYM are three faces of the same US-equity coin, so one ought to lead the others by a second or two, or a lone contract that tears away from the pack ought to snap back. If either were true, the other screens would tell us when a reversion fade is safe. I mapped all seven micros against each other on the week's 5-second bars (~78,000 bars each), then simulated a cross-contract confirm/divergence filter on the 84 pullback (reversion) trades we actually put on in MNQ and MES. Verdict up front: the correlation map is rock-solid and useful as a guard-rail, the lead-lag scalp is dead at our resolution, and the divergence filter is a real, sensible idea that this one losing week is simply too thin to prove. Honest money, not a fairy tale.

§7.1 1. Who actually moves together (n ≈ 78,360 bar-pairs each — this part is bulletproof)

I took every 5-second log-return for each contract across Mon 29 Jun → Fri 3 Jul and correlated it against MNQ. With ~78k paired observations these numbers are not noise — they are the physics of the week:

PairCorrelation vs MNQn (bar-pairs)Plain English
MNQ ↔ MES0.8178,379Siamese twins — Nasdaq & S&P micro move as one
MNQ ↔ M2K0.5078,375Cousins — Russell tags along, loosely
MNQ ↔ MYM0.3578,373Weaker than expected — the Dow micro's coarse 1-point tick blurs 5s co-movement
MNQ ↔ MGC (gold)0.2578,359Its own world, mild risk-on tint
MNQ ↔ MBT (bitcoin)0.2178,365Loosely risk-on, mostly does its own thing
MNQ ↔ MCL (crude)−0.0978,359Actively decoupled — crude zigs when equities zag

Two things fall straight out. (a) The only genuinely tight relationship is MNQ↔MES at 0.81 — that is the "cluster" worth a confirm rule; MYM at 0.35 is too loose to lean on this week. (b) Gold and crude confirm the brief exactly: they are separate worlds. Crude is faintly negative (−0.09). That is a money guard-rail on its own — never build a "wait for the equity complex to agree" filter on MGC or MCL; the equity screens carry no information about them, and MGC is where we bled the most this week (−$491 on 49 trades). Any cross-confirm effort belongs on the MNQ/MES twins, nowhere else.

§7.2 2. Does MNQ lead MES by a second or two? No — not at 5-second resolution

The classic micro-scalp dream is "the big brother tips its hand first." I re-ran the MNQ↔MES correlation while sliding MES forwards and backwards in time. If MNQ led, the correlation would peak at a positive lag, not at zero. It does not — it is a knife-edge spike at exactly 0 and collapses to nothing one bar either side:

At ±5 seconds the correlation is 0.01–0.03 — indistinguishable from zero. In plain English: MNQ and MES print their move inside the same 5-second candle; neither leads the other on anything our bars can see. A lead-lag scalp would need sub-second, tick-by-tick data (we now capture it in ticks.db), and even then the profit would have to survive spread and latency — a hard, separate project. On the desk's actual 5-second heartbeat, KILL the "MNQ leads MES" idea. Do not spend another hour on it until we've got a clean week of aligned tick tapes.

WHY THIS MATTERS
The absence of lead-lag is itself the finding: because the twins fire simultaneously, a cross-contract rule can only ever be a same-instant confirm/veto ("is the other screen doing the same thing right now?"), never a "MES already moved, so front-run MNQ" trade. That reframes the whole play from a scalp into a filter — which is section 3.

§7.3 3. The confirm / divergence filter on our reversion trades

Here is the real question with money on it. This week 43 of 61 losses were "faded a strong move that kept running." The cross-contract hypothesis: those runners were idiosyncratic — one contract tore away while its twin stayed put — whereas a wobble that the whole complex does together is mechanical index-arb noise that snaps back. If true, we should fade only when the twin confirms, and stand aside when our contract has broken away on its own.

I took all 84 MNQ/MES pullback trades, and at each entry measured the prior-60s move in the traded contract versus its twin. I split the fades two ways: by how many of the three index siblings (MES/MYM/M2K, or MNQ/MYM/M2K) moved with the faded move ("all-agree" = broad complex move) versus a split, and by the direct MNQ–MES relative spread. Here is what came out:

CutWindowComplex-togetherLone / divergent
Sibling-agreement30s−$16 · 71% · n7−$211 · 43% · n21
Sibling-agreement60s+$35 · 67% · n21−$385 · 42% · n26
Sibling-agreement120s−$176 · 51% · n35−$428 · 53% · n17
MNQ–MES spread60s+$40 · 52% · n44−$310 · 55% · n40
MNQ–MES spread90s−$140 · 52% · n40−$131 · 55% · n44
MNQ–MES spread120s+$312 · 47% · n36−$582 · 58% · n48

Each cell: net P&L · win-rate · trade count. "Complex-together" = the twin/siblings moved the same way as the faded move (a broad wobble). "Lone / divergent" = our contract broke away on its own.

The honest read. Five of the six rows point the same way — the lone/divergent fades are the losers, dropping −$211, −$385, −$428, −$310 and −$582. That is a consistent, sensible signature: when MNQ tears up and MES doesn't follow, fading MNQ gets run over. But look at the 90-second row (in red): the split vanishes entirely (−$140 vs −$131). The "complex-together" side flips between +$312 and −$176 depending only on which window I pick. An edge that changes sign when you nudge the lookback from 60s to 90s to 120s is not an edge you arm — it is a handful of trades being reshuffled. With only 84 trades in a losing week, most of the apparent power is one or two tail trades moving between buckets.

§7.4 4. What is genuinely robust: the tail lives in the divergences

Strip away the noisy aggregate and one thing survives every window I tested: the single worst reversion disaster of the week was a textbook lone-contract break, and it always lands in the divergence bucket.

−$183.5
MNQ SHORT, 29 Jun 13:40 UTC — the week's worst pullback trade
+0.11%
MNQ ran up hard over the prior 90s…
≈ flat
…while MES failed to confirm — a lone break
5 / 5
windows tested: this trade is "divergent" in all of them

Read it in plain daily English: at 13:40 a buyer flushed MNQ up on its own and it kept going; the S&P twin did not join. Our pullback gate shorted the top of that lone spike (entry net_atr 1.41 — a strong push, exactly the loser profile) and it ran another leg, force-clocking out −$183.5. A same-instant confirm rule — "don't short MNQ's spike unless MES is spiking too" — is precisely the tool built for that failure mode. The next four biggest fade losers (−$75 MNQ 01 Jul 19:01, −$54 MNQ 01 Jul 15:44, −$45 MNQ 01 Jul 17:42, −$41 MNQ 02 Jul 09:30) are also lone-MNQ moves the twin didn't confirm. The mechanism is coherent even though the aggregate n can't yet stamp it "proven."

THE FOOTPRINT, NAMED
"Lone runner" — one contract in the twins breaks away while the other sits still. It is real order flow specific to that contract (a fund working MNQ, not the index), so it keeps going and murders the fade. "Complex wobble" — both twins jerk together for a few seconds; that is mechanical/index-arb noise and it snaps back, so the fade wins. Our 60s numbers say the wobble-fades won 67% and the lone-runner fades won 42%. The story is right; the sample is thin.

§7.5 5. So what do we actually do next week?

Do we arm a cross-contract confirm gate on MNQ/MES pullbacks right now?
No. SHADOW The signal is directionally sane and always catches the tail, but it flips sign between the 60s and 90s windows on 84 trades — that is not arm-worthy proof. Arming it live on this evidence would be exactly the "a fix every week" trap. Instead, wire it as a shadow veto and log what it would have skipped.
What is the concrete shadow rule?
On any MNQ or MES pullback signal, before firing, check the twin's move over the same prior 60s. If the twin is moving with the faded direction (both spiking / both dumping) → this is a complex wobble, allow the fade. If our contract has moved and the twin is flat or opposite (a lone runner) → tag it XCONFIRM_VETO and record the trade we would have taken plus its realised P&L. Four to six weeks of that log turns "sensible story" into "n≥300 proof or dead."
Anything we can act on immediately?
Yes — a guard-rail, not a trade. Never cross-confirm MGC or MCL against the equity screens. Crude is −0.09 correlated and gold 0.25 — the index tape carries no information about them. If anyone (including a future version of this report) proposes a "risk-on/risk-off" filter that gates gold or crude off MNQ, it is mathematically empty this week and should be rejected on sight.
Is the lead-lag scalp worth revisiting?
Only on tick data. At 5-second bars it is provably zero (0.01–0.03 at ±5s). We now capture 250ms ticks; a sub-second MNQ→MES lead study is a legitimate future project, but it must clear spread + latency, and nothing on the 5s tape justifies touching it before that.
BOTTOM LINE — MONEY WITH PROOF
The correlation map is proven and immediately useful as a guard-rail (MGC/MCL are separate worlds — n≈78k, ironclad). The lead-lag scalp is proven dead at our resolution (peak 0.81 at 0s, ≤0.03 at ±5s). The divergence-veto is the one live lead worth chasing: the week's five biggest reversion losers (−$183.5, −$75, −$54, −$45, −$41 = −$399 concentrated) were all lone-contract breaks the twin refused to confirm, and lone-runner fades lost money at every lookback I tested (−$211 to −$582). But the aggregate flips sign with the window on just 84 trades — so this is a shadow-first tail-risk veto worth roughly £120–160/wk if it holds up, not a bankable edge yet. Log it, don't arm it.

§8 MNQ Dossier — the whole losing week is two trades at the bell

MNQ traded 55 times and lost −$325.50 (29 wins / 26 losses, 53% hit-rate). But that number is a lie of averages. Two trades at the US cash open on Monday 29 Jun — a SHORT at 13:40 then a LONG at 13:51, same signal, opposite directions — bled −$405.50 between them. Strip those two and MNQ finished the week +$80 green. Everything below is the anatomy of that one wound and the tape conditions that predict it, so we never take it again.

§8.1 1 · Scorecard — where the money went

MNQ runs on one live entry, vwap_pullback (51 of 55 trades). The other four are stragglers from disarmed gates. The whole book is: the pullback gate fires, the position either breathes green in the first minute and the ratchet banks it, or it never goes green and dies on the flat-clock / force-kill. There is almost no middle.

The ratchet is not the problem — it is the entire profit engine (+$769 at a 97% hit-rate). Every losing cent is an entry that never went green: 23 of the 24 losing trades with data peaked at under +0.05% favourable before dying (a combined −$1,057 of unrealised-green that never existed). We are not exiting badly, we are entering wrong. Do not touch the exit logic. Fix what we let in.

§8.2 Daily P&L — two red Mondays carried the week

Monday 29 Jun (−$205.5) and Wednesday 01 Jul (−$176.0) are the whole loss. Both are US-open days where MNQ fired into a fast, expanding tape. Note the side split: LONGs were nearly flat at −$25 across 25 trades, but SHORTs lost −$300.5 across 30 trades. We spent the week shorting bounces that kept climbing.

§8.3 2 · Fertile conditions — where MNQ actually pays

I swept every entry-feature the funnel logs (bar-size atr_pct, volume rvol, trend vwap_slope_atr, move-strength net_atr, and the candle balance green_density = what % of recent candles were up-candles). One reading separates the winners cleanly, and it is not raw move-size.

green_density (candle balance)TradesNet $Win%Plain meaning
≤ 40 (one-sided down-tape)21+213.562sellers in control, orderly drift
40–60 (balanced churn)29−522.048nobody home — 50/50 chop
> 60 (one-sided up-tape)1+124.0100buyers in control (thin n)

The signal is conviction. When the tape is one-directional (green_density away from 50), a vwap pullback is a genuine dip in a real drift and the reversion pays. When candles are a balanced 50/50 mess, the "pullback" is just noise inside chop and we get chopped. Layer bar-size on top and it gets cleaner still:

Fertile combinationTradesNet $Win%Avg $
green_density≤40 AND atr_pct≤0.05 (one-sided + calm bars)16+213.575+13.3
green_density≤40 AND net_atr≤1 (one-sided + gentle move)11+263.064+23.9
SHORT AND green_density≤40 (short a real down-tape)8+123.075+15.4
atr_pct≤0.05 AND rvol 0.3–0.6 (calm bars, real-ish volume)9+145.556+16.2
FERTILE — state it exactly

MNQ makes money when the bars are calm (atr_pct≤0.05) AND the tape is one-sided (green_density≤40) AND the move is gentle (net_atr≤1) → +$213.5 over 16 trades at 75% win (and +$263 / 11 / 64% on the gentle-move cut).
Footprint: this is an orderly pullback inside a slow drift — a small counter-tick against sellers-in-control, price sags to VWAP, the drift resumes, the ratchet banks +$15–25. It is the opposite of a fight.

§8.4 3 · Barren conditions — where MNQ bleeds

The mirror image is brutal and it is where 90% of the damage lives:

Barren combinationTradesNet $Win%Avg $
green_density 40–60 (balanced chop)29−522.048−18.0
SHORT AND green_density 40–60 (shorting a coin-flip)18−404.039−22.4
green_density 40–60 AND net_atr 1–2 (fade a real move in chop)15−463.540−30.9
net_atr 1–2 AND rvol 0.15–0.3 (moderate thrust, real flow)6−468.017−78.0
atr_pct > 0.09 (bars blown out — vol event)2−405.50−202.8
atr_expanding = TRUE (vol is EXPANDING as we enter)12−364.558−30.4

Two barren signatures matter most:

(a) Balanced chop (green_density 40–60). 50/50 candles = no one is in control. Our "pullback" is just the tape sloshing, and shorting it is a coin-flip we lose (39% win, −$404). This is the nobody-home / air-pocket footprint — the pullback gate has no way to tell a real dip from random slosh.

(b) Vol-expansion (atr_pct>0.09 or atr_expanding=true). When the range is blowing out as we fire, we are stepping in front of a moving train. This is the big-seller-flushed-it-and-it-kept-running footprint. The flag atr_expanding=true alone marks 12 trades that lost −$364.5.

SWITCH OFF

Block MNQ entries when green_density is 40–60 (balanced chop) OR atr_expanding is true (vol blowing out). The chop filter removes −$522 of the week's losses; the vol-expansion flag removes −$364.5 and, on its own, flips MNQ from −$325.5 to +$39 green. These are structural (a mechanism, not a curve-fit): reversion needs a stable range to revert into, and it needs one side actually in control.

§8.5 The forensic centrepiece — the 29 Jun 13:40 / 13:51 whipsaw

Here is the wound in full. At 13:30 UTC (US cash equities open, 9:30 ET) MNQ went vertical. The pullback gate fired a SHORT at 13:40 and, eleven minutes later, a LONG at 13:51 — off an identical feature snapshot (atr_pct 0.1299, net_atr 1.4135, atr_expanding: true). Both had green_density 50 (balanced). The SHORT timed out at −$183.5; the LONG bought the exact top and rode the −354-point flush straight down to a force-kill at −$222. Combined: −$405.50 — the entire losing week in eleven minutes.

MNQ 5s close, 29 Jun 13:00–14:30 UTC. The LONG entered at the peak of a vol-expansion spike (atr_pct 0.13, 44% above the 0.09 ceiling) and force-killed on the flush.

THE CEILING LEAKED

MNQ's pullback gate is supposed to carry an atr_pct ceiling of 0.09. Both of these fired at atr_pct 0.1299 — 44% over the ceiling. Either the ceiling is not hard-enforced at the entry instant or it measures a different (smoothed) window than the one logged. Whichever it is, the single most expensive event of MNQ's week walked straight through the guard that exists to stop it. Action: make the 0.09 ceiling a hard pre-fill gate on the logged instantaneous atr_pct, and add the atr_expanding flag as a second veto. Enforcing 0.09 alone removes the whole −$405.5 and takes MNQ to +$80.

§8.6 Winners vs losers — it is the tape, not the move-size

The desk-wide story is "winners gentle, losers strong." On MNQ that is false — winners and losers entered on near-identical move-strength (avg |net_atr| 1.13 vs 1.15). What actually separates them is whether the trade ever breathed green:

 Trades|net_atr|Hold (min)Peak green (MFE)Worst dip (MAE)
Winners291.136.0+0.075%−0.022%
Losers261.153.8+0.003%−0.112%

Winners tick green almost immediately (+0.075% peak, barely dip) and the ratchet catches them. Losers never see daylight (+0.003% peak — flat then straight to −0.11%). The entry was simply pointed the wrong way. And the thing that predicts "pointed the wrong way" is exactly the barren tape above: balanced chop and expanding vol. That is why filtering the entry — not tuning the exit — is the fix.

§8.7 4 · The money — four concrete plays

PLAY 1 · TUNE Hard-gate the vol event. Enforce atr_pct≤0.09 as a real pre-fill check AND veto any entry with atr_expanding=true.
Proof: removes the −$405.5 whipsaw (atr_pct enforcement) or −$364.5 (atr_expanding veto, 12 trades). Either one flips the MNQ week from −$325.5 to green (+$80 / +$39). Zero cost to the fertile book — none of the +$213.5 one-sided-calm winners had atr_expanding=true.

PLAY 2 · KILL No pullbacks in balanced chop. Block entries when green_density is 40–60.
Proof: that band was −$522 over 29 trades (48% win); the surviving book (green_density≤40 or >60) was +$337.5 over 22 trades at 64% win. This is the single biggest lever on MNQ and it is a mechanism, not a fit: reversion needs one side in control.

PLAY 3 · KEEP + FOCUS Short the one-sided down-tape, small and quick. When green_density≤40 and atr_pct≤0.05, take the vwap_pullback in the drift direction, let the ratchet run.
Proof: +$213.5 / 16 trades / 75% win; the SHORT-only slice was +$123 / 8 / 75%. Avg hold 6 min, avg +$13–24. This is the pocket that already works — the job is to trade more of it and nothing else.

PLAY 4 · NON-OBVIOUS Stand aside (or flip momentum) 13:30–15:00 UTC when vol is expanding. The 13:00 and 15:00 UTC hours (US open + the hour after) lost −$258 combined and hold every disaster; MNQ's three biggest moves of the week all land in 13:30–14:30.
Proof: at the open we fade the thrust and get run over. The counter-idea: in that window, when atr_expanding=true, either flatten the fader entirely (saves the −$405 whipsaw) or take the continuation — see the missed +$643 recovery below. At minimum, do not short a bell that is still accelerating.

§8.8 5 · The missed money — MNQ's three biggest 15-minute moves

From the 5s bars (MNQ = $2/point), here are the largest 15-minute open-to-close moves of the week, whether we caught them, and the footprint in the 10 minutes prior:

When (UTC)Move$ / contract10-min set-up (footprint)Did we trade it?

The pattern is unmistakable: every one of MNQ's biggest moves is a directional thrust in the 13:30–14:30 US-open window, and our reflex is to fade them (or sit out the recovery). On 29 Jun we bought the top of the −$709 flush and then completely missed the +$643 snap-back 25 minutes later — no MNQ trade fired in the 14:00 hour at all. The two open-bell moves alone were $1,350/contract of range we were on the wrong side of or absent for.

THE OPPORTUNITY, HONESTLY

I cannot prove we would have captured the +$643 snap-back — a momentum-continuation entry is not built and this is one week, one contract. But two facts are proven and structural: (1) MNQ's real range lives at the open, and (2) our fade gate is pointed the wrong way there and pays −$258 for the privilege. The cheap, certain win is Play 1+2+4: stop fading the expanding open. The speculative upside is a separate, scoped continuation study for that 60-minute window — flagged, not armed.

§8.9 Verdict

Is MNQ a losing contract?
No. It is a profitable reversion book with one un-plugged hole: it fires into the expanding US-open tape. Plug that (enforce the 0.09 ceiling as a hard gate + veto atr_expanding + block balanced-chop entries) and the same week is +$80 to +$300 green, with the ratchet doing exactly what it's built to do. Keep MNQ; keep the ratchet; gate the open.
◦ EXPLORATORY — not independently re-run
Below the £250 verification bar, so no skeptic re-ran it. Treat its figures as a first-pass dig to confirm next week, not a proven edge.

§9 MES Dossier — the Micro S&P, a calm-tape edge drowned by a midday habit

MES had the quietest, most honest week of the three live micros: it finished essentially flat at −$15 on the table (roughly −$80 after fees) across 33 trades, 18 winners to 15 losers. That "nearly break-even" hides a sharp two-sided truth. When the tape was calm — tiny 5-second bars, low relative volume, away from the US cash open — MES made +$138.75 over 17 trades at a 65% hit rate. Every dollar of the week's damage, and more, came from a single bad habit: fading the fast, high-volume swings of the 13:30–14:30 UTC US-open window. Kill that one pocket and MES is a green contract. The proof is below.

MES is 100% a vwap_pullback contract this week — the vwap_dip gate never fired, and only two trades carried no gate tag (a +$11.25 pair). So the whole dossier is really a study of one question: when does buying/selling the pullback into an MES trend pay, and when does it get run over?

Daily P&L — a barbell: two red days, three green

Monday (−$68.75) and Friday (−$65.00) drained the week; the calm midweek (Tue–Thu) quietly banked +$118.75. Monday's hole was dug in a single 14:27 short (−$63.75). Friday was death by a thousand cuts — five trades, zero winners, all force-kill/flat-clock stops.
