§17.1 Where the money died, day by day

Monday alone lost -$457 — more than the other four days combined. That is not bad luck; it is one specific thing. The gold line marks Tuesday morning, when the calm-market ceiling (atr_pct 0.09) went live on MNQ and MES. Before the line the desk let itself trade violent, blown-out bars; after it, it didn't. Look what happens to the red bars once the line passes.

READ THIS FIRST

Only three contracts traded live this week: MGC -$491 (49 trades), MNQ -$326 (55 trades), MES -$15 (33 trades). And it is lopsided by direction: short trades lost -$655 across 86 trades, longs only -$177 across 51. We spent the week selling into moves that were going up and buying into moves that were going down — i.e. standing in front of the tape. That single habit is the spine of every autopsy below.

§17.2 The cause of death, tallied

Every loss falls into one of five buckets. I sorted each trade into the first bucket it matches, worst-diagnosis-first, so no trade is double-counted. Two buckets — violent bar and strong-fade — hold more than 60% of the money.

#Cause of death — in plain EnglishTradesLostThe fix
1Got in on a violent bar — bar-size (atr_pct) above 0.09, the market was ripping, no edge to a mean-reversion entry4-$508already fixed on MNQ/MES
2Faded a strong move that kept going — the move's strength (net_atr) was 0.30+; a big seller/buyer was in control and we bet against it29-$996strength ceiling
3Never went green, bled slow — wrong from the first tick, then held 6-15 min before the clock cut it7-$186faster red cut
4Went green, then handed it all back — showed real profit (0.05%+ in our favour) and we let it round-trip into a loss4-$255tighter ratchet
5Clean fade that just didn't come back — calm entry, reasonable setup, market simply kept drifting. The honest cost of doing business17-$501fair loss

Read that table like a doctor: causes 1 and 2 are 33 trades and -$1,504 — that is the tumour. Cause 5 (17 trades, -$501) is the one we can't cut without also killing our winners — it's the tax a reversion desk pays for being early. Causes 3 and 4 (-$441) are exit problems, not entry problems, and are covered in the exits section; I flag them here only so the tally is honest and adds to the full -$2,446.

§17.3 Why fading strength is the killer — the proof in one table

Here is the whole book (not just the losers) sliced by how strong the move was at the moment we entered — that's the "net_atr" number, and in plain terms it's "how many normal bars' worth of push was already in the tape, in the direction we're betting against." Small number = a gentle drift we can lean on. Big number = a freight train we're standing in front of.

Strength of the move we fadedTradesWin %Net $Avg/trade
0.00 – 0.10  gentle drift, nobody in charge5360%-$13-$0.2
0.10 – 0.20  mild450%+$18+$4.4
0.20 – 0.30  firm1100%+$8+$8.0
0.30 – 0.45  strong push on333%-$46-$15.3
0.45+  freight train — a big player is flushing it6551%-$534-$8.2

The line is stark. Everything at strength under 0.30 nets roughly flat-to-green (+$13 across 58 trades). Everything at 0.30 and above nets -$580 across 68 trades. The win rate barely moves (51% vs 57%) — this isn't about being wrong more often, it's that when you fade a freight train and lose, you lose big (the average loss is fat because the move keeps running past your stop). That is the single most important sentence in this autopsy.

THE MONEY, PROVEN

Add one rule: if the move's strength (net_atr) is 0.30 or more, or the bar is violent (atr_pct > 0.09), don't take the fade. On this exact week that skips 68 trades that together netted -$580 (34 of them were winners, but as a group they lost). The week goes from -$832 to -$252 — a +$580 swing — and you never touched the calm book that actually pays. That is roughly +$580/week, the biggest single lever in this whole section.

§18 Autopsies — the worst losses, one body at a time

The blue dot is where we got in; the gold dashed line is our entry price; the red dot is where we bailed. Each ends in a verdict and, where one exists, the simulated dollars a rule would have saved.

§18.1 MNQ · the Monday 13:40 triple-whipsaw · -$332 net in 24 minutes

29 Jun · one violent bar (atr_pct 0.13, thin volume rvol 0.28) · three trades

What happened: MNQ went haywire on a single blown-out bar. We caught the first leg up nicely — long at 29,683, ratchet banked +$74. Then, near the top, the desk shorted the rip at 29,695 — and the buyers kept coming, +90 points against us, flat-clock cut it for -$184. Two minutes later it bought the very top at 29,825, the bar rolled over and collapsed 110 points, force-kill cut it for -$222. We got run over from both sides of the same spike.

Verdict: Never trade a bar this violent. The bar-size at entry was 0.13 — 50% over the 0.09 ceiling. Both losers would have been blocked by the calm-market ceiling that went live the very next morning. Saved: +$406 on these two alone. already fixed on MNQ/MES

§18.2 MGC · the four-hour round-trip · -$57

30 Jun 09:30 UTC · SHORT · held 240 min · showed +0.26% in profit · exit SIGNAL_SELL

What happened: This one was right. The short worked — gold fell all the way down and we were up 0.26% (a clean +$100-ish) at the bottom of the chart around the middle of the window. Then we sat in it for four hours, watched the whole move retrace, and closed below our entry for -$57. A textbook "won the trade, lost the money."

Verdict: Nothing wrong with the entry — this is an exit failure. A profit-lock that banks even a third of a 0.26% winner turns -$57 into roughly +$30. This is the "went green, gave it back" bucket, and it is why the exit overhaul matters as much as the entry filter. tighter ratchet / profit-lock

§18.3 MGC · the slow morning bleed · -$105

30 Jun 07:44 UTC · LONG · held 106 min · showed +0.22% then reversed · exit FLAT_CLOCK

What happened: Same disease, opposite direction. We were long, gold dipped first (the entry looks bad early), then rallied and we were up +0.22% around the middle. From there it ground sideways-to-down for over an hour and the flat-clock finally cut it well underwater at -$105 — the biggest MGC loss of the week. The chart is a mountain: we rode all the way up the near side and all the way down the far side without ever ringing the register.

Verdict: Another green-then-red round-trip. Bank a slice of the +0.22% and this is a small winner instead of the day's worst gold loss. tighter ratchet / profit-lock

§18.4 MGC · the honest one — a clean fade that just didn't come back · -$61

29 Jun 19:23 UTC · SHORT · held 7 min · calm entry, never green · exit STOP_LOSS

What happened: Nothing dramatic — and that is the point. Calm tape, a sensible short into a small pop, and the market simply kept grinding up. It never went green, the stop did its job in 7 minutes, and we paid -$61. No blown-out bar, no freight train, no giveback.

Verdict: No rule catches this and none should. This is the -$501, 17-trade "cost of being a reversion desk" bucket. If we filter hard enough to skip trades like this, we skip the winners that look identical at entry. Take the loss and move on. fair loss — part of the game

§19 The three rules that would have caught most of them

Across all 61 losses, three structural rules — not lazy threshold nudges, but rules with a mechanism behind them — cover 40 of the 61 losers and -$1,690 of the -$2,446. Here they are, each with what it catches and the money.

§19.1 Rule 1 — Don't fight a freight train (strength ceiling)

If the move's strength at entry (net_atr) is 0.30 or more, skip the fade. Mechanism: a strength of 0.30+ means a real, one-sided flow is in control — a big seller flushing it or a big buyer lifting it — and a mean-reversion entry is betting that flow reverses in the next few minutes. It usually doesn't; it runs you over. Catches 29 of the pure strong-fade losers (-$996), and the whole 0.30+ band nets -$580 across 68 trades even after counting its winners. TUNE — add a strength ceiling to the pullback gate

§19.2 Rule 2 — Don't trade a violent bar (extend the 0.09 ceiling to gold)

If the bar is blown out (atr_pct > 0.09), skip. This ceiling already went live on MNQ and MES on Tuesday — and it worked: every single above-0.09 trade this week happened on Monday, before it deployed; zero fired after. Those Monday trades cost -$466 on MNQ/MES (including the -$222/-$184 whipsaw pair). The one gap: MGC still has no ceiling, and one uncapped MGC trade at atr_pct 0.131 lost -$39 this week. Thin on its own, but it is the same mechanism and the same fix — put the 0.09 ceiling on gold too. TUNE — extend atr_pct 0.09 cap to MGC

§19.3 Rule 3 — Bank the winners you're already handing back (exit, not entry)

This one doesn't skip a trade, it changes the exit — so it costs zero winners. 16 losers this week went green first and died red, for -$827; 45 losers never went green at all and averaged 8 minutes of dead-money bleed. The MGC four-hour round-trip and the 106-minute mountain above are the poster children. A profit-lock that banks a fraction of any trade that shows real green, plus a faster cut on trades that are never green, attacks the -$441 (buckets 3+4) directly. Detail and the sim live in the exits section; flagged here because half the autopsies are exit stories wearing an entry costume. TUNE — profit-lock + faster red cut

THE HONEST LIMIT

Be clear-eyed: the entry filters do not make this week profitable — they make it small. Skipping the freight-trains and violent bars lifts the week from -$832 to -$252. The remaining -$252 sits in the calm book, and it is an exit problem (giveback) plus the irreducible -$501 of clean fades that no honest rule can remove without also cutting the winners. So the prescription is two-handed: Rule 1+2 stop the big bleeds next week (~+$580), and the exit work claws back the givebacks. Neither alone flips the sign; together they do.

If I could only flip one switch on Monday, which is it?
Rule 1 — the strength ceiling. It is worth roughly +$580 on this exact week, it has a real mechanism (don't bet against a flow that's still running), and it barely touches the calm book that pays. The gold-bar ceiling (Rule 2) is a cheap, obvious add-on to a filter that already proved itself on MNQ/MES.
Isn't "skip the strong moves" just "trade less"?
No — and I checked. If it were just trading less, the calm book would lose too. It doesn't: the under-0.30 band is +$13 across 58 trades while the 0.30+ band is -$580 across 68. We're not cutting volume at random; we're cutting the specific, mechanism-backed slice where fading loses fat. The 17 clean fades prove the discipline: those we keep, because they're the same shape as our winners.

How We Catch the Big Misses (idle gates, simulated)

◦ HONEST NULL — no net-positive rule this week
The lab found nothing that pays net of costs — meaning the desk's current refusal here is correct. A real null is a result, not a failure.

§20 Missed-Move Capture Lab — MNQ (Nasdaq micro)

The report already showed the big MNQ moves we sat out this week. This section answers the only question that matters next time: how do we get involved — mechanically, on every fire, winners and losers — and does it actually pay? We have three idle shadow gates that could chase these moves: momentum (ride a thrust), ORB (09:30 ET opening-range break) and dip (fade a flush). I built each as a concrete rule with exact triggers and simulated it across the whole week on 5-second bars, net of a full tick of spread plus fees ($1.50 round-turn on MNQ). The honest verdict up front: all three lose money this week. The gate is right to refuse them. Here is the proof, loser by loser.

HEADLINE
Across 285 mechanical fires this week, not one of the three capture rules is net-positive after costs. Momentum bleeds −$732 (n=175), the dip/flush fade is a chop-magnet at −$1,472 (n=102, 26% win), and ORB structurally can only fire ~once a day so it never gets enough shots (n=8, −$48). MNQ trades live, so these are real shadow tests — and the shadow says stand down. The money in MNQ momentum lives in the desk's selectivity (see the Ride-Strength section), not in blindly firing on every thrust.

§20.1 1) The ten biggest 15-minute moves — and what they actually were

These are MNQ's ten largest 15-minute displacements this week (measured on 5s closes, sized in money at $2/point), with the desk's footprint reading just before each one. Read the "type" column first, because it explains everything that follows:

When (UTC)Move $PtsPlain-English typervolVWAP dist (ATR)Opening range

The single most important fact on this page: every one of the ten biggest moves happened in the 13:22–14:48 UTC window — the first 75 minutes of the New York cash session (09:22–10:48 ET). And six of the ten are reversal bounces (V-snaps and inverted-Vs) — the tape ripping one way then instantly ripping back. Look at Monday: +$488 up, then −$750 straight back down, then +$638 back up, all inside 80 minutes. That is not a trend to ride; it is a whipsaw meat-grinder. Any mechanical rule that fires into that window — and all three of ours do — is walking into the exact place designed to chop it up. That is the whole story of why they lose.

§20.2 2) The three capture rules — exact triggers

No hand-waving. Each rule has a concrete trigger, exit and stop, mapped to its idle gate. Simulated on 5s bars during the NY session (13:30–19:55 UTC), one position at a time, 5-minute cooldown after each exit, costed at $1.50 round-turn (one tick of spread + $1.00 fees).

Rule → gateEnterExit / stop
MOMENTUM → momentumPrice travels ≥1.5×ATR in the last 5 min with rvol≥1.2 → enter with the thrust.Give-back trail (exit on 40% pullback of peak profit); hard stop 1×ATR.
ORB → ORBBreak of the 09:30–09:35 ET (13:30–13:35 UTC) first-5-min range by ≥0.25×ATR, once per side per day.Same give-back trail; hard stop 1×ATR.
DIP / FLUSH → dipSharp flush ≥1.5×ATR down in ≤5 min on rvol≥1.2, price ≥1×ATR below VWAP → buy the snap-back (mirror for spikes up).Target = VWAP; hard stop 1×ATR.

§20.3 3) The simulation — every fire, all week

This is the honest scoreboard: every time each rule would have fired this week, winners and losers, net of cost. "Caught on big moves" is how much of the ten headline moves above the rule actually banked — the thing it was supposed to capture.

§20.4 The cumulative curves — all three head down

Each line is one rule's running net P&L over its fires in time order (Mon 29 → Fri 3). There is no ambiguity here: none of them turns the corner. The dip fade (red) is a straight slide to −$1,472; momentum (blue) grinds sideways-to-down to −$732; ORB (grey, eight dots) barely moves.

Cumulative net $ per rule, fires in time order. ▬ red = DIP/flush fade (→ −$1,472)   ▬ blue = MOMENTUM ride (→ −$732)   ▬ grey = ORB (→ −$48, 8 fires). Dashed = break-even.

§20.5 Why each one loses — the mechanism, not the excuse

DIP / FLUSH fade (−$1,472, the worst). This is the clearest failure and the most dangerous idea. It wins only 26% of the time, and the payoff shape is lethal: the wins are capped tiny (target is VWAP, ~+$10 to +$20) while the losers run to the stop and beyond — the five worst single fires were −$121, −$117, −$114, −$113, −$113. When MNQ flushes 1.5 ATR on heavy volume it is usually flushing for a reason, and buying that knife catches the falling part far more often than the bounce. It actually lost −$394 on the big moves it was built to catch. This is the live vwap_pullback/vwap_dip disease in its purest mechanical form. The gate refusing it is not caution — it is correct.

MOMENTUM ride (−$732). More respectable — 57% win, and its winners can be real (+$142, +$92) — but it still bleeds, because most 1.5-ATR thrusts on MNQ are the first leg of exactly the V-snap whipsaws in the table above. You enter with the thrust, the give-back trail hands back 40% of the good ones, and the false thrusts (74 of 175 fires lost) nickel-and-dime it under water. Tellingly, this "capture" rule caught only +$75 of the ten headline moves — it was rarely even pointed the right way when they happened. I swept it hard (thrust 1.0/1.5/2.0 ATR × rvol 1.0/1.2/1.5, plus fixed 5/10/15-min holds): the natural give-back-trail grid is negative in all nine combos (−$694 to −$792). One fixed-hold variant (15-min clock, 1.5-ATR stop) scraped +$348 and an all-hours 10-min version +$146 — but their immediate neighbours are deeply negative (the same setup at a 10-min hold loses −$323, at a 1.0-ATR stop −$624). That is a knife-edge fit, not an edge. I am not promoting it.

ORB opening-range break (−$48). Least bad in dollars, but it fails on a different axis: it can only fire about once per side per day, so a five-day week gives it eight shots total. Eight fires, 62% win, still −$48 net, and no amount of parameter-nudging (buffers 0.10 / 0.25 / 0.50 ATR all land between −$45 and −$72) changes that. With n=8 it could never be a shadow candidate this week even if it had squeaked positive — there simply is not enough evidence. The morning break on MNQ this week was more often the start of a reversal than a clean run.

§20.6 4) Verdict

SHADOW DECISION — MNQ
None of the three capture rules is net-positive across the week after costs. Momentum −$732, dip/flush −$1,472, ORB −$48. The gate is right to refuse all three. Do not arm any of them.

The nuance worth carrying forward: MNQ momentum does pay when it rides the desk's own selected entries (+$418 in the Ride-Strength section, on 31 gate-chosen trades) — but that edge lives in the gate's selectivity and timing, not in a blind "fire on every 1.5-ATR thrust" rule, which dilutes to a loss. The right next step is ride what the gate already picked, not chase every move on the tape. No new shadow candidate from this lab.

RuleDecision

DIP / FLUSH (dip gate)DON'T ARM −$1,472, 26% win chop-magnet
ORB (ORB gate)DON'T ARM −$48 and only n=8 — never enough shots

§21 MES Missed-Move Capture Lab — can we mechanically get involved next time?

The dossier showed the big MES moves we sat out. This lab asks the only useful follow-up: if we bolt a concrete rule onto one of the three idle shadow gates — ride-the-thrust (momentum), opening-range breakout (ORB), or fade-the-flush (dip) — and fire it every single time it triggers all week, not just on the three moves it was designed around, do we actually make money? Simulated on 5s bars, net of a 1-tick spread ($1.25) plus fees ($1.00) = $2.25 round-trip per contract. MES is a live contract, so these are true shadow tests against the current gate.

Verdict up front

All three capture rules lose money across the week. Momentum comes closest (−$89, 56% win) but is a slow chop-magnet that peaks at +$188 mid-week and hands it all back. Fade-the-flush is a falling-knife disaster (−$635, 26% win). ORB barely fires and bleeds. No shadow candidates this week — the gate refusing these moves is correct. The MES edge is a calm-tape reversion edge; these are all momentum-chasing rules that fight it.

A move that snaps up then rolls over is death to a momentum-follower and a lottery for a dip-fader. This is the core reason mechanical capture is so hard here.

§21.2 2 · The three capture rules, stated exactly

Momentum-continuation (momentum gate): when price has travelled ≥1.5 ATR in the last 5 min on rvol ≥ 1.2, enter WITH the thrust. Exit on a 40% give-back trail off the peak, hard stop 1.0 ATR, 30-min cap, 5-min cooldown. RTH only.

ORB-breakout (ORB gate): after the 09:30–09:35 ET opening range is set, enter the first break of the range by ≥0.25 ATR in the break direction (one long + one short max/day). Same 40% trail / 1 ATR stop.

Dip / flush-bounce (dip gate): after a sharp ≥1.5 ATR flush in ≤5 min on rvol ≥ 1.2 that leaves price ≥1 ATR from VWAP, fade back toward VWAP (buy the flush / sell the spike). Target = session VWAP, stop 1.0 ATR, 20-min cap.

§21.3 3 · Fire it every time, all week — the simulation

RuleGateFiresWin%$/fireNet $/wkCaught on the big movesVerdict
Momentum 1.5ATR/5minmomentum18356−0.49−89+260KILL
ORB 0.25 ATR breakORB850−5.00−40−2KILL
Dip fade → VWAPdip9726−6.55−635−168KILL

Cumulative net P&L per fire, in order, across the week. The zero line is dashed. Notice the momentum curve (green) climbs to +$188 by Wednesday then bleeds the lot back on false triggers; the dip-fade (red) is a near-monotonic slide — every flush it buys keeps flushing.

§21.4 4 · Why each one fails, and why the gate is right

Momentum KILL — it did catch +$260 of the ten headline moves, proving the trigger finds real thrusts. But firing 183 times a week means ~37 signals a day, and MES thrusts revert fast: the give-back trail books tiny winners while the losers run to the 1-ATR stop. Peak +$188 → close −$89. Widening or tightening the ATR/rvol thresholds (tested 1.0–2.0 ATR × 1.0–1.5 rvol) never turns it positive; the best variant is still −$82. It is a chop-magnet, not an edge.

ORB KILL — only 8 fires all week (below the n≥8 bar, and it needs to clear the bar with profit, not just meet it). 50% win, −$40 net, and it caught essentially none of the big moves (−$2): this week's opening-range breaks were mostly head-fakes that reversed into the inverted-Vs above. Loosening the buffer to 0.10 ATR makes it worse (−$55); tightening to 0.50 ATR trims the loss but not to profit (−$32).

Dip / flush-fade KILL — the worst by a mile: −$635, 26% win, −$168 given away on the big moves it tried to fade. Buying a ≥1.5 ATR flush on rising volume is textbook catching a falling knife — the volume that makes the flush "sharp" is exactly the volume carrying it further. Every threshold (1.2 / 1.5 / 2.0 ATR) loses; requiring a bigger 2.0-ATR flush only cuts the bleed to −$261.

Bottom line for MES

None of the three capture rules is net-positive this week, so there are no shadow candidates and £0/wk of honest capture money. The idle gates are right to refuse these moves. MES's real edge (per the dossier) lives in quiet tape and mean-reverts; bolting a momentum-chaser or a knife-catcher onto it fights that edge and gets shredded on the 4×–21× rvol false triggers that dominate its big moves. Re-run next week — a genuine trend day could flip momentum — but on this reversal-heavy week the answer to "how do we get involved?" is "we don't, mechanically, yet."

◦ PROPOSED, UNPROVEN — ~£97/wk but below the £250 skeptic bar
Not independently re-run. Treat as exploration to confirm next week, not a proven edge.

§22 Missed-Move Capture Lab — MGC (micro gold)

The report already showed the big MGC moves we sat out. Here we stop admiring them and ask the only question that matters: can we mechanically get involved next time and keep the money? Three idle shadow gates get the same brutal test — ride a thrust (momentum), break the 09:30 ET opening range (ORB), buy a flush (dip). Every rule is fired across the whole week on 5s bars, winners and losers, net of ~1 tick slippage + fees (~$2.30/round-trip). No rule is scored only on the moves it was built to catch.

Bottom line

Two of the three rules are pure chop-magnets on MGC this week and lose money exactly as the live gate’s refusal predicts. The dip/flush-bounce is a knife-catcher (−$289, 32% win). The base momentum-chase gets shredded on false thrusts (−$248 over 86 fires). Only one config squeaks green: momentum, but ONLY on the most explosive thrusts (≥2 ATR in 5 min on rvol ≥5×) — +$123/wk over 27 fires. But be honest: a single 06-29 short (+$137) IS the whole week; strip it and it’s −$14. Thin, tail-dependent, one contract-week. MGC’s momentum/ORB/dip gates are in shadow, so this is a shadow-on-shadow read.

§22.2 2 — Three concrete capture rules (exact triggers)

A. Momentum-continuation (momentum gate) — enter WITH a thrust: last 5 min moved ≥ T ATR AND rvol ≥ R. Give-back trail: arm at +0.5 ATR favorable, exit on 40% give-back of peak; hard stop 1 ATR; max hold 30 min; 5-min cooldown. Tested at T=1.5/R=3 (base) and T=2.0/R=5 (explosive-only).

B. ORB-breakout (ORB gate) — opening range = high/low of 13:30–13:35 UTC (first 5 min after 09:30 ET). First break of that range by >0.1 ATR, in the break direction, between 13:35–16:00 UTC; one shot per day. Same trail; hard stop = opposite side of the range.

C. Dip/flush-bounce (dip gate) — after a sharp flush of ≥1.5 ATR down in ≤5 min on rvol ≥3 while price is below VWAP, buy the snap-back. Stop below the flush low −0.5 ATR; 40% give-back trail; max hold 20 min.

The dip rule only turns green if you starve it to n=6 (rvol≥5 → +$164) — below the n≥8 bar and pure over-fit. ORB is directionally clean (5/5 wins) but a 5-day week gives at most 5 fires; it cannot reach n≥8 and stays unproven.

§22.5 4 — Verdicts

KILL Dip/flush-bounce. −$289 over 19 fires, 32% win, and it lost −$148 on the very big moves it was built for — MGC’s flushes (event-driven crude-correlated gold selloffs) keep going, they don’t snap back. The live gate refusing to buy MGC dips is correct.

KILL Momentum base (rvol≥3). −$248 over 86 fires. Moderate thrusts on MGC are noise; you get chopped on the false starts faster than the real thrusts pay. Refusal correct.

WATCH, don’t arm Momentum explosive (2 ATR / rvol≥5). Net-positive (+$123, n=27, 56% win) so it technically clears the bar — added to shadow_candidates — but it is single-trade-dependent (strip one 06-29 short and it’s −$14) and untested out of sample. Keep it in shadow and require it to prove itself over more weeks before any live consideration.

UNPROVEN ORB-breakout. 5/5 wins, +$70, and the two largest moves of the week broke the 09:30 ET range upward — the cleanest story here. But a 5-day week caps it at 5 fires; it cannot reach n≥8 and is not promotable on this sample. Re-run over a multi-week window before judging.

Honest read: for MGC this week, three capture rules and essentially zero robust edge. The desk sitting out these moves cost real money on paper, but every mechanical way to get in either loses (dip, base momentum) or hangs on one lucky trade (explosive momentum). The gate’s caution on gold is doing its job.

§23 Missed-Move Capture Lab — M2K (Russell 2000 micro)

The report already showed the biggest M2K moves we sat out this week. This section answers the only question that matters next time: how do we get involved — mechanically, on every fire, winners and losers — and does it actually pay? Three idle shadow gates could chase these moves: momentum (ride a thrust), ORB (09:30 ET opening-range break) and dip (fade a flush). I built each as a concrete rule with exact triggers and simulated it across the whole week on 5-second bars (78,436 M2K bars, Mon 06-29 → Fri 07-03), net of a full tick of spread plus fees ($1.54 round-turn: one 0.10-pt tick = $0.50 at $5/point, plus ~$1.04 commissions). M2K is observe-only — it took zero live trades this week — so this is a shadow-on-shadow test.

HEADLINE
Two of the three rules lose money in every variant I tried and the gate is plainly right to refuse them: the ORB break bleeds −$177 (n=10, best-tuned still −$27) and the dip/flush fade is a falling-knife magnet at −$209 (n=73, 37% win) — and the flush-fade loses even though it was built to catch the two single biggest moves of the week. The momentum ride is the one survivor, but only after you loosen it below a real thrust: as designed (≥3 ATR/5min) it loses −$74; loosened to ≥2 ATR it clears the bar at +$167 (n=59, 46% win). Honest caveat — strip its three best fires and it is −$26, and it flips negative under a tight trail. It goes to shadow as WATCH-don't-arm, not as an edge.

§23.1 1) The ten biggest 15-minute moves — and what they actually were

M2K's ten largest 15-minute displacements this week, measured on 5s closes and sized in money at $5/point, with the footprint reading in the 10 minutes before each. The "type" column is the story: five clean breakdowns, three breakouts, three reversal bounces — and the two biggest of all were reversal bounces (V-snaps off a flush), exactly the moves the dip rule targets.

Three facts that decide everything below. (1) M2K's "big" moves are small in dollars — the largest 15-minute swing all week was $135 on one contract, and the median 1-minute ATR is just 0.74 pt ($3.71). Against a $1.54 round-turn cost, the edge-to-noise ratio is thin from the start. (2) Three of the ten biggest moves happened before 09:30 ET (08:15, 09:15, 09:15) — the opening range does not even exist yet, so ORB structurally cannot catch them. (3) The two largest moves were low-warning V-snaps (a −1.1% stretch below VWAP snapping +$135 back; a 7×-rvol flush reversing +$101) — beautiful in hindsight, but every quiet drift and slow bleed also looks like the front half of one until it isn't. That is why fading flushes mechanically loses.

§23.2 2) The three capture rules — exact triggers

Momentum-continuation (gate: momentum). On 5s bars, measure the thrust = close now minus close 5 minutes (60 bars) ago. Fire with the thrust when |thrust| ≥ 2.0 × ATR and rvol ≥ 1.5. Hold on a give-back trail of 1.2 ATR off the running peak; hard stop 2.0 ATR; max hold 20 min; 20-min cooldown after each exit. Entry window 08:00–16:00 ET.

ORB-breakout (gate: ORB). Build the 09:30–09:35 ET (13:30–13:35 UTC) high/low. Fire on the first break each direction per day when close crosses the range edge by >0.25 ATR; stop the far side of the range; give-back trail 1.5 ATR; hold to 16:00 ET.

Dip/flush-bounce (gate: dip). After a sharp ≥1.5 ATR flush over 5 min and price stretched ≥1.0 ATR past VWAP on rvol ≥ 1.2, fade back toward VWAP (buy the down-flush, short the up-spike); target = VWAP touch; hard stop 1.5 ATR; give-back trail 1.2 ATR.

§23.3 3) Simulated across the whole week — every fire, winners and losers

Rule (as stated)n firesNet $Win %Avg $/fireVerdict
Momentum as designed (thrust≥3 ATR, rvol≥1.5)43−$7435%−$1.72KILL
Momentum loosened (thrust≥2 ATR, rvol≥1.5)59+$16746%+$2.83WATCH

The momentum survivor's per-fire and cumulative curve. Left chart: every one of the 59 fires (green = winner, red = loser), a sea of small red losses punctuated by a handful of fat green wins — the classic momentum signature. Right chart: cumulative equity, which is a staircase up on three or four steps, not a smooth climb.

Per-day, momentum earns on the trending days and gives it back on the chop: Mon +$23 (12 fires) · Tue −$42 (11) · Wed +$54 (14) · Thu +$153 (13) · Fri −$21 (9). Two of five days are net red, and Thursday alone is more than the whole week's profit.

§23.4 4) Verdicts

KILL ORB-breakout. −$177 over 10 fires (30% win); even the best of four buffer/trail variants only claws back to −$27, still a loser and only 8 fires. Two structural problems: M2K's opening range is narrow, so a 0.25-ATR buffer triggers on the first fake-out and stops out on the reversal; and three of the ten biggest moves were pre-09:30 ET, before any range exists — ORB cannot touch them by construction. The gate refusing M2K opening breaks is correct.

KILL Dip/flush-bounce. −$209 over 73 fires (37% win), negative in all five variants tested (−$143 to −$222). This is the cruel one: the two largest moves of the week were exactly the V-snaps this rule targets (+$135 and +$101), yet firing on every qualifying flush means buying dozens of falling knives that keep falling. On a low-dollar micro like M2K, the snap-backs it does catch are too small to pay for the flushes that don't reverse. The gate refusing to fade M2K flushes is correct.

WATCH, don't arm Momentum-continuation (loosened). +$167 over 59 fires, 46% win, average +$2.83 against a $1.54 cost. It clears the bar (net-positive, n ≥ 8) so it goes to shadow_candidates — but with three honesty flags stapled to it: (1) the rule as designed — a real ≥3-ATR thrust — loses −$74; the profit only appears when you loosen the thrust filter to near-noise, which is a warning, not a green light. (2) Strip the three best fires and it is −$26 — the entire week's edge rides on three catches (the Mon 09:56 −$102 short and two Thu breakouts). (3) It is fragile to the exit: a tight 0.8-ATR give-back trail flips it to −$147 — it only works if you give momentum a wide leash. It survives cost stress (still +$108 at a doubled $2.54 cost), so the tail is real, but one week and three decisive trades is a thin reed. Keep it in shadow, demand it repeat over multiple weeks before any live consideration.

Honest read for M2K: of three ways to get involved, two lose in every form and are rightly refused, and the third only pays by loosening below a genuine thrust and leaning on three lucky catches. This is a $135-max-move, $3.71-ATR micro — the moves are real but small, and the cost-to-signal ratio punishes chasing. The desk sitting these out cost little on paper and the gate's caution is doing its job. The one thread worth pulling is wide-leash momentum, and only in shadow.

◦ HONEST NULL — no net-positive rule this week
The lab found nothing that pays net of costs — meaning the desk's current refusal here is correct. A real null is a result, not a failure.

§24 Missed-Move Capture Lab — MYM (Micro Dow)

MYM traded zero times live this week (us_futures_daytrade, 0 rows since 2026-06-29) — it is observe-only, so everything below is a shadow-on-shadow test: would arming the idle momentum / ORB / dip gates on MYM have caught the moves we sat out? I pulled all 78,435 MYM 5s bars for the UTC week, found the ten biggest 15-minute moves, then simulated three concrete mechanical capture rules across the whole week — every fire, winners and losers — net of ~1 tick spread + fees.

The verdict is blunt: all three rules lose. The gate refusing these moves is right. MYM's multiplier is only 0.5 (1 point = $0.50), so a full 200-point move is worth just $100, while the round-trip cost (1 tick $0.50 + ~$0.50 fees = $1.00) eats the edge. The dominant missed move is not a clean trend to ride — it is a sharp flush that snaps back up in the first 45 minutes of the 09:30 ET cash open, the single hardest thing to chase mechanically without getting stopped on the wrong side first.

§24.1 1) The ten biggest 15-minute moves we sat out

Size in money = points × 0.5. Footprint (rvol, net_atr) is the live funnel reading in the 10 min before; VWAP distance and ORB position computed from the 5s bars. Note the clustering at 13:30–14:15 UTC = 09:30–10:15 ET, the RTH open, and the recurring shape: price flushes below VWAP / below the opening range, then rips back up.

6 of the 10 are the same animal: a flush-and-snapback-up at the open (highlighted). Two are up-breakouts, one a down trend-continuation, one a down reversal. Tellingly, the pre-move rvol is mostly below 1.0 — these moves did not announce themselves with a volume surge, which is exactly why a volume-gated capture rule struggles to see them coming.

§24.2 2) Three concrete capture rules (exact triggers)

Rule A — Momentum-continuation (gate: momentum). Trigger: trailing 5-min move ≥ 1.5 ATR with funnel rvol ≥ 1.0 → enter WITH the thrust. Exit: give-back trail (arm after +1 ATR MFE, exit on 40% retrace of peak), hard stop 1.5 ATR, 30-min time cap, 15-min cooldown.

Rule B — ORB-breakout (gate: ORB). Trigger: build the 09:30 ET first-5-min range (13:30–13:35 UTC); after 13:35, first break of the range by ≥ 0.25 ATR in the break direction → enter that way. Exit: stop = opposite side of the opening range, give-back trail 40%, 60-min cap, one long + one short per day.

Rule C — Dip / flush-bounce (gate: dip). Trigger: trailing 5-min flush ≤ -1.5 ATR, price below VWAP, rvol ≥ 1.0 → buy the first green 5s bar (the snap-back). Exit: target toward VWAP capped at +1 ATR, stop -1 ATR, 20-min cap, cooldown.

§24.3 3) Full-week simulation — every fire, net of costs

RuleFiresNet $Win%Top moves caught$ on thoseVerdict
A momentum-continuation224-320524 / 10+66KILL
B ORB-breakout8-196625 / 10-193KILL
C dip / flush-bounce68-44532 / 10-12KILL

Rule A is a classic chop-magnet: 224 fires, 52% win, a steady bleed to -$320. It caught 4 of the big moves for +$66, but paid it all back — and then some — on 220 false thrusts that faded. Chasing a 1.5-ATR pop on MYM mostly buys the top of a wiggle.

Rule B (ORB) loses on its shorts. Only 8 fires, 62% win — but the two down-breaks (06-29 14:14 short -$113.50; 07-01 13:36 short -$132.50) were exactly the flushes that snapped back UP, and the fixed opening-range stop is wide, so each reversal was a big loss. The five small longs (+$20, +$10, +$11, +$22, +$13) can't cover two -$120 shorts. This is the same pathology already logged for the ORB gate: it shorts rising bounces.

Rule C (dip-bounce) is the least-bad — and still loses. It is the rule that matches the dominant missed-move shape, yet across 68 fires it nets -$44 (53% win). Crucially its gross P&L before costs is only +$24 — the entire thin edge is inside the $1.00/round-trip cost. On MYM's 0.5 multiplier, costs are the whole story.

§24.4 4) Did any tuning save the dip rule? (diligence)

I swept the flush size and volume filter to check whether a stricter, better-motivated dip rule pays. It does not hold up:

Dip variantnNet $Gross $Win%
flush≥1.5 ATR, rvol≥1.0 (base)68-44+2453
flush≥1.5 ATR, rvol≥1.554+34+8857
flush≥1.5 ATR, rvol≥2.045-33+1256
flush≥2.0 ATR, rvol≥1.549-3+4655
flush≥2.5 ATR, rvol≥1.536-8+2853
Why the +$34 cell is NOT a shadow candidate

Exactly one cell prints positive (flush≥1.5 / rvol≥1.5: +$34, n=54). It is a fragile island: drop the volume filter to 1.0 and it goes to -$44; raise it to 2.0 and it goes to -$33. Non-monotonic, negative on both neighbours — the signature of an overfit fluke, not an edge. Its per-fire net is +$0.63, smaller than the $1.00 cost assumption itself, so a two-tick spread on a fast open flips it red. Per the shadow-candidate bar (net-positive AND robust), this fails robustness and I refuse to promote it — it would be stamped REFUTED on re-run. Every momentum variant stayed net-negative regardless of threshold.

§24.5 Bottom line

None of the three capture rules is net-positive this week. The idle momentum, ORB, and dip gates refusing MYM are correct.

The moves were real and large in points, but MYM's 0.5 multiplier makes them small in money, and the dominant shape — an open-bell flush that reverses — is precisely the one that whipsaws a mechanical entry: momentum buys the top, ORB shorts the bottom right before it rips, and the dip-bounce only breaks even before costs. There is no shadow candidate to add from MYM. Best net-positive capture rule this week: $0/wk. If anything ever earns a look here it is the flush-bounce family, but it needs a genuinely lower cost basis (tighter spread capture) and a fresh week to clear the $1.00 hurdle — today it does not.

Caveats: one UTC week; 5s bars; ATR from 1-min(14), VWAP anchored to the 22:00 UTC globex session, ORB to 13:30–14:15 UTC = 09:30 ET; costs modelled at 1 tick ($0.50) + $0.50 fees = $1.00 round-trip per 1-lot. MYM is observe-only, so this is a shadow-on-shadow read.

◦ HONEST NULL — no net-positive rule this week
The lab found nothing that pays net of costs — meaning the desk's current refusal here is correct. A real null is a result, not a failure.

§25 Missed-Move Capture Lab — MCL (micro WTI crude)

The desk sat out MCL's biggest moves all week — and the honest answer to "how do we get involved next time?" is: on this week's tape, we shouldn't. I took the three idle shadow gates — momentum-continuation (ride a thrust), ORB (09:30 ET opening-range break) and dip/flush-bounce — wrote each as a concrete mechanical rule, and fired it on every 5-second bar from Mon 29 Jun to Fri 03 Jul, winners and losers, net of ~1 tick of spread plus fees (~$2.20 round-trip). All four configurations lose money. Not one is net-positive. A 144-config parameter sweep can't find a single winning momentum setting either. MCL is observe-only, so this is a shadow-on-shadow test — and the verdict is that the gate refusing these moves is doing its job.

Setup

MCL multiplier = 100 ($1.00 of price = $100). Median 1-minute ATR this week ≈ 0.053 in price = $5.34 per contract — crude is roughly twice the intraday range of the equity micros. 78,428 five-second bars. Price ranged 67.05–71.55 ($450/contract top-to-bottom). Cost model per round trip: ~1 tick spread (0.01 = $1.00) + fees (~$1.20) = $2.20.

§25.1 1 — The ten biggest 15-minute moves we sat out

Ranked by money (displacement × 100), non-overlapping. The plain-English move type and the 10-minute footprint before it. Note the clock column: nine of the ten biggest MCL moves happen in the European morning / pre-US-open (06:00–13:00 UTC) — crude is a London-hours, inventory-and-geopolitics market. Only one big move (01 Jul 14:39) even occurs after the 09:30-ET opening range forms.

When (UTC)Move $ΔPrice rvolnet ATRvs VWAP Plain typeORB?

The pattern: the money is in $50–63 directional bursts, split fairly evenly between up-breakouts and down-breakouts, most on only modest rvol (0.7–2.0) — they aren't screaming volume spikes you can key off cleanly. Several biggest moves even fired on below-average volume (rvol 0.7). That is the first warning that a "wait for a volume-confirmed thrust" trigger will be late and noisy on this contract.

§25.2 2 — The three capture rules (exact mechanics)

MOMENTUM-continuation → momentum gate. Trigger: price moves ≥ 1.0 ATR in the trailing 5 minutes on rvol ≥ 1.5. Enter with the thrust at the bar close. Exit: 40% give-back trail off peak, hard stop 1.0 ATR, 30-minute time cap. (Also tested a strict variant: ≥1.5 ATR / rvol ≥ 2.0.)

ORB-breakout → ORB gate. Build the 09:30-ET (13:30 UTC) first-5-minute range. After 13:35 UTC, enter on the first close beyond the range by > 0.1 ATR in the break direction. Stop = opposite side of the range; target = 1.5× range; day time-cap 20:00 UTC. One shot per day.

DIP / flush-bounce → dip gate. After a sharp ≥ 1.5 ATR move in 5 minutes on rvol ≥ 1.5, fade it back toward VWAP: enter when price snaps 0.2 ATR off the extreme (long after a flush, short after a spike, only while still on the far side of VWAP). Target VWAP, stop 0.3 ATR beyond the extreme, 20-minute cap.

§25.3 3 — Simulated across the whole week (every fire, net of costs)

RulenNet $Win% Avg $/fireBig moves hit$ caught on the big movesVerdict
Momentum (1.0 ATR / rvol 1.5)180−42533−2.49 / 10−9KILL
Momentum-strict (1.5 ATR / rvol 2.0)94−20635−2.28 / 10+15KILL
ORB 09:30-ET break5−1160−2.20 / 100KILL (n<8)
Dip / flush-bounce to VWAP126−10937−0.98 / 10−27KILL

Read the momentum row carefully, because it is the whole story. The rule does get involved: it fires inside 9 of the 10 biggest moves. But the money it caught on those moves is −$9 — the 40% give-back trail hands back the burst, and the other 171 fires are false thrusts that get chopped. Even the tightest-triggered strict variant that keys off the 8 big moves only nets +$15 on them, and still loses −$206 overall on the chop tax. The dip rule is the same disease with a smaller stop: it catches 8 of the moves but bleeds −$109 net at −$0.9/fire. Both are textbook chop-magnets.

Momentum cumulative P&L (180 fires, left→right): a straight bleed to −$425, no equity plateau where it "works."

Dip/flush cumulative P&L (126 fires): same shape, ending −$109 (dipped to −$158 mid-week).

§25.4 4 — Verdict: the gate is right to refuse these

To be sure I wasn't just unlucky with parameters, I swept the momentum rule across 144 combinations of thrust size (0.8–2.0 ATR), rvol floor (1.2–3.0), give-back trail (25–60%) and stop (0.8–2.5 ATR). The best config with n≥8 still loses −$34 (n=37, 62% win); the worst loses −$646. There is no net-positive corner of the space this week.

Bottom line

None of the three capture rules is net-positive on MCL this week — so none is a shadow candidate. Three concrete reasons, not hand-waving:

1. Wrong clock. 9 of the 10 biggest moves are pre-US-open (European session); the 09:30-ET ORB gate literally cannot see them and fires only 5 times all week (−$11).

2. The bursts don't announce themselves. Several of the biggest moves ran on below-average volume (rvol 0.7–1.2), so a volume-confirmed thrust trigger arrives late and mostly fires on false ones — 171 of 180 momentum fires were not big moves.

3. Crude's ~$5.34 ATR eats the give-back. Even when momentum caught the real move, the trail handed back essentially all of it (−$9 net on 9 hits); wider stops just enlarge the per-fire loss. This matches the standing read that MCL is a different beast — 2× the equity-micro range, event-driven — and every reversion/continuation gate fires it into its own bleed-zone.

Keep MCL observe-only. This was a shadow-on-shadow test and all four rules earned a KILL. Revisit only if we (a) re-anchor an opening-range rule to the London/Brent-crude open rather than 09:30 ET, and (b) have dense tick fills to price the ~1-tick crude spread honestly — on 5s bars the spread assumption is already generous, and it still loses.

◦ PROPOSED, UNPROVEN — ~£89/wk but below the £250 skeptic bar
Not independently re-run. Treat as exploration to confirm next week, not a proven edge.

§26 MBT Missed-Move Capture Lab — can we mechanically get involved next time?

The dossier showed the big Micro-Bitcoin moves we sat out. This lab asks the only useful follow-up: if we bolt a concrete rule onto one of the three idle shadow gates — ride-the-thrust (momentum), opening-range breakout (ORB), or fade-the-flush (dip) — and fire it every single time it triggers all week, not just on the moves it was designed around, do we actually make money? Simulated on 5s bars, net of ~1 tick spread ($0.50, MBT tick = 5 pts × $0.10 mult) plus fees ($1.00) = $1.50 round-trip per contract. MBT traded 0 times live this week — it is observe-only, so everything here is a shadow-on-shadow test: a hypothetical gate on a contract we do not yet arm.

Verdict up front

Two of the three rules are chop-magnets that lose: momentum bleeds a near-monotonic −$197 (117 fires), ORB is −$13 on only 10 fires. But the third — fade the euphoric up-spike back to VWAP — is genuinely and robustly net-positive. The naive 1.5-ATR version is razor-thin (+$45, dies if costs rise), but requiring a deeper 2.0-ATR thrust and taking only the short (sell-the-spike) side nets +$113 / 27 fires / 74% win, and stays positive even at a pessimistic $2.50 round-trip. That is a real SHADOW CANDIDATE — the one honest way to get involved in MBT's big moves. Buying the down-flush (catching the knife) loses; the edge is entirely in fading exuberance.

§26.1 1 · The ten biggest 15-minute MBT moves this week — and what they were

Move size in money = points × $0.10 multiplier. Footprint columns are the 10 minutes before the move: rvol (10-min volume vs the week's typical 10-min bucket), net displacement in ATRs, distance from session VWAP in ATRs, and whether price broke the 09:30 ET opening range (13:30 UTC). The 5-min ATR runs ~104 pts (~$10.40).

The read: MBT's biggest moves cluster in two places — the RTH open (13:30–14:20 UTC) on high rvol, and the thin 21:00–22:30 UTC crypto-close window where rvol collapses to 0.1–0.6 and price whips. Notice the pairs: 21:33 spike +$166 then 21:48 reversal −$145, and 21:46 spike +$108 then 22:01 reversal −$83. Price rockets 4–10 ATRs above VWAP on almost no volume, then snaps back. That inverted-V — euphoric up-thrust far from VWAP, then mean-reversion — is exactly the footprint a sell-the-spike fade is built to harvest, and it is where the only edge on this contract lives.

§26.2 2 · The three capture rules, stated exactly

Momentum-continuation (momentum gate): when price has travelled ≥1.5 ATR in the last 5 min on rvol ≥ 1.2, enter WITH the thrust. Exit on a 40% give-back trail off the peak, hard stop 1.0 ATR, 30-min cap, 5-min cooldown.

ORB-breakout (ORB gate): after the 09:30–09:35 ET (13:30–13:35 UTC) opening range is set, enter the first break of the range by ≥0.25 ATR in the break direction (one long + one short max/day). Same 40% trail / 1 ATR stop / 30-min cap.

Dip / flush-fade (dip gate): after a sharp ≥1.5 ATR thrust in ≤5 min on rvol ≥ 1.2 that leaves price ≥1 ATR from VWAP, fade back toward VWAP (buy a down-flush / sell an up-spike). Target = session VWAP, stop 1.0 ATR, 20-min cap. The tuned variant below tightens the thrust to 2.0 ATR and takes the sell-the-spike side only.

§26.3 3 · Fire it every time, all week — the simulation

RuleGateFiresWin%$/fireNet $/wkCaught on big movesVerdict
Momentum 1.5ATR/5minmomentum11754.7−1.68−196.9−8.4KILL
ORB 0.25 ATR breakORB1040.0−1.27−12.7+27.5KILL
Dip fade 1.5ATR → VWAP (both sides)dip10948.6+0.41+45.1+20.0TUNE
Dip fade 2.0ATR → VWAP, sell-spike onlydip2774.1+4.19+113.2+22.1CANDIDATE

Cumulative net P&L per fire, in order, across the week (zero line dashed). The momentum curve (red) is a textbook chop-magnet — it slides almost monotonically to −$197 as MBT thrusts revert into its 1-ATR stop. The dip-fade curve (green, the robust 2.0-ATR sell-spike variant) climbs steadily to +$79 on the symmetric book and +$113 on the short-only book, with the two big up-spike reversals (06-29 & 07-01 close) as its standout catches.

§26.4 4 · Robustness — is the dip edge real, or one lucky config?

The naive both-sides 1.5-ATR fade (+$45) is not safe to trust: at a $2.00 round-trip it flips to −$9, so it is one bad-fill assumption from breakeven. The edge survives only when you make two honest cuts, and it survives them everywhere:

VariantnNet @$1.00Net @$1.50Net @$2.00Net @$2.50Win%
1.5-ATR flush, both sides109+99.6+45.1−9.4—49
2.0-ATR flush, both sides48+94.9+79.3+55.3—58
2.0-ATR flush, sell-spike only27+126.7+113.2+99.7+86.274
buy-the-flush leg alone (1.5-ATR)45−9.6 (@1.5)———44

Two clean findings. (1) The edge is directionally asymmetric: fading up-spikes back to VWAP works (74% win), buying down-flushes is knife-catching and loses (−$10). The volume that makes a flush "sharp" is exactly the volume carrying it further — but a low-volume euphoric spike 4–10 ATRs above VWAP has nothing behind it and reverts. (2) Requiring a deeper 2.0-ATR thrust keeps only the extreme dislocations, lifting gross-per-fire to ~$5.7 — big enough that even a pessimistic $2.50 crypto-overnight spread leaves +$86 on the table. That cost-robustness is the whole reason to trust it: MBT's overnight spread genuinely can widen to several ticks, and this rule absorbs it.

§26.5 5 · Why momentum and ORB are right to be refused

Momentum KILL — 117 fires, −$197, and it caught essentially none of the headline moves (−$8). MBT thrusts revert violently (that is the whole dip-fade thesis in reverse), so a with-the-thrust entry books tiny trailed winners and eats full 1-ATR stops when the spike snaps back. The curve never recovers.

ORB KILL — only 10 fires (borderline on the n≥8 bar, and it must clear the bar with profit), 40% win, −$13. It did catch +$27 of the real RTH breakouts, proving the trigger isn't blind — but this week's opening-range breaks were mostly head-fakes that reversed, so the false triggers outweighed the catches. Not an edge on this sample.

Bottom line for MBT

Momentum and ORB are chop-magnets — the idle gates are right to refuse them. But there is one honest way to get involved: fade the euphoric up-spike back to VWAP — sell a ≥2.0-ATR / rvol≥1.2 thrust that leaves price ≥1 ATR above VWAP, target VWAP, 1-ATR stop, 20-min cap. It nets +$113/wk (27 fires, 74% win) ≈ £89 at brief cost, and stays positive to a pessimistic $2.50 spread. This mechanism is the same quiet-tape reversion the desk already trusts elsewhere, pointed at MBT's overnight exuberance. Caveat, loudly: MBT is observe-only, so this is a shadow-on-shadow result on a single week and a small contract — promote it to a live shadow, not a live position, and re-confirm the fill assumptions against real MBT overnight spreads before it earns any size.

§27 Greenfield Signal Lab — MNQ: the runs the faders can't catch

Every gate on this desk is a fader — it sells stretch and buys weakness, betting on the snap-back. That means the money it structurally cannot touch is the opposite animal: the directional run that keeps going. This section ignores the existing gates entirely and asks one question — MNQ printed eight fat 15-minute moves this week; was there anything in the tape 20–30 minutes beforehand that would have let us stand in front of them? The honest answer is a qualified yes, but thin: the up-runs share a real, mechanical fingerprint (a volume-confirmed thrust), a signal built on it clears +$998 net across the whole week firing 62× — but the profit leans on a fat tail and two trend days, and it bleeds on the whippy open. It is a shadow candidate, not a green light. I killed three prettier mirages on the way, and I show you the graves.

§27.1 1. The eight moves we missed

Top eight non-overlapping 15-minute MNQ moves this week, measured close-to-close on 5-second bars, money at the ×2 multiplier. Tick coverage (data/ticks.db, 250 ms) begins 06-29 18:00 UTC, so the three 06-29 daytime moves are dissected on 5s bars + funnel only; the rest have full microstructure.

#Start (UTC)Move$ (×2)ShapeTicks?
106-29 13:53−374.8 pt−$750exhaustion reversal off the highno
207-02 14:13−324.0 pt−$648range-break down, continuationyes
306-29 14:18+318.8 pt+$638V-snap up (bounce off the crash low)no
406-29 13:30+243.8 pt+$488cash-open volume explosion (6.6×)no
506-30 13:22+240.5 pt+$481dead coil → break on 1.7× volyes
607-02 14:38−223.0 pt−$446exhaustion reversal (vol dropping)yes
706-29 14:48+215.5 pt+$431volume-expansion break up (1.4×)no
807-02 13:38+201.8 pt+$404volume builds → break up against down-slopeyes

The clock is loud: every one of the eight lands between 12:21 and 15:16 UTC — the US cash-open hour and the first two hours after it. Overnight Globex, for all its tick coverage, produced no move this big. Whatever signal we build only needs to be awake for a three-hour window.

§27.2 2. Dissecting the precursor — two different animals

Slicing the 20 minutes before each move on 5s bars + the funnel features split the eight cleanly into two families:

Family A — the volume-confirmed thrust (the up-runs, #4,5,7,8). These are catchable. The fingerprint: the tape goes quiet (low volume, a tight or fading range), then a burst of participation drives price through the recent extreme.

  • 06-30 13:22 (+$481) is the storybook case. For 20 minutes MNQ coiled in a 39-point range on ~128 contracts/bar (atr_pct 0.045 — volatility crushed). Then a single 5s bar broke the coil high on 1.7× the coil's volume, and price ran +215 points past the break.
  • 06-29 13:30 (+$488) is the same shape at higher octane: the prior 10 minutes ranged just 30 points on 217 contracts/bar, then the 13:30 cash open detonated — the first two minutes traded 6.6× that volume (1431 vs 217/bar) and price ripped up through the whole overnight range.
  • 07-02 13:38 (+$404) shows the build: volume in the last 10 minutes rose to 1167/bar from a 694 baseline, and the break itself ran 2.0×. Its funnel vwap_slope_atr was −0.95 — the desk's own trend read was pointing down as price broke up. This is precisely why the fader gates and the trend-shadow both miss it: it is a reversal against the visible slope, confirmed only by the volume surge.

Family B — the exhaustion reversal (the biggest down-moves, #1,6). These are not catchable by a volume trigger. Before the −$750 at 06-29 13:53, price was pinned at the top of its range (position-in-range 0.93) and the reversal fired on falling volume (0.77× the prior average). The −$446 at 07-02 14:38 likewise broke down on 0.75× volume. There is no participation spike to key on — buyers simply evaporate and price sags. A momentum trigger arrives late and wrong; these are closer to what the faders are supposed to handle, or they are simply news-driven air.

The precursor, drawn: 06-30 13:22, coil then break

Blue = 5s close; grey = per-bar volume; yellow band = the 39-point coil; red dashed = the break bar (volume spikes ~3× against the coil floor, price leaves and does not come back).

§27.3 3. Two graves — the mirages I killed before they reached the skeptic

The precursor looks like a compression breakout, so I tested the obvious signal — and it failed. Being honest about this is the whole point.

Grave #1 — the naive volume breakout is a coin-flip

Rule: a 5s bar closes above the prior 5-minute high on ≥3× average volume. It fires 335 times this week. Forward 15-min return: −2.0 pt, 47% win. Both directions, every threshold from 2.5× to 4×: nothing. Most range-breaks on MNQ fail and revert — which is exactly why the faders earn their keep. A single break bar is noise.

Grave #2 — "tighter coil = cleaner break" is backwards

I binned all 1,213 volume-breakouts by how tight the preceding coil was. The tightest quartile (6–16 pt range) was the worst: −2.4 pt, 41% win. The widest quartile was the best (52% win). Micro-tight coils on 5s bars are just chop that snaps back the instant it pokes out. The 39-pt coil at 06-30 was a genuine winner, but "find the tightest coil and buy the break" is a curve-fit onto that one memory. Killed.

What survived was a whisper of genuine momentum. Across ~7,800 samples, a strong 5-minute thrust (top decile, ≥36 pt) continues in the same direction over the next 15 minutes 54% of the time (correlation of past-to-forward return +0.03). Tiny — but 54% with asymmetric pay-off (winners bigger than the stopped losers) is exploitable. The trick is that a tight stop destroys it: every stop of 12–18 pt got whipsawed and lost −$1,400 to −$2,300 on the week. The continuation is real but it wiggles hard on the way; you must give it room.

§27.4 4. The signal: Volume-Confirmed Thrust-Continuation

Built on Family A's fingerprint, symmetric so it can also short the down-breaks (#2). Fully mechanical on 5s bars:

ComponentRule
ThrustNet move over trailing 5 min (300s) ≥ 40 pt in one direction
ParticipationMean volume of the last 60s ≥ 1.5× the thrust window's own baseline (the surge, not a static level)
ExtensionCurrent 5s bar closes further in the thrust direction than the prior bar (still pushing)
EntryMarket at the trigger bar close, in the thrust direction
Stop40 pt adverse (wide — ~1× the thrust; give it room to wiggle)
ExitTime-stop at 15 min, else the 40-pt stop; 3-min cooldown between fires
Costs0.75 pt round-trip charged (1 tick slip + fees; observed spreads 0.25–0.75 pt)

No compression filter (Grave #2), no tight stop (it whipsaws), no clock filter — the volume surge does the time-of-day gating for free, because the surges happen at the open.

Cumulative net P&L, in order of fire. The story is written into the curve: it bleeds all through the whippy 06-29 open down to −$474 (momentum enters wrong on both legs of a V-reversal day), then the trending 07-01 and 07-02 sessions carry it to a +$1,112 peak before settling at +$998.

§27.6 6. Verdict — a thin candidate, honestly labelled

SHADOW This meets the letter of a new-edge candidate: it fires 62× (far beyond its 8 design moves), stays net-positive (+$998) after realistic costs, n ≥ 8, and the long/short symmetry argues against a one-week directional accident. That is a real, mechanical fingerprint the desk's faders structurally cannot see.

But I will not oversell it, because the skeptic is right to push:

  • Tail-and-trend dependent. Strip the three biggest winners and the week nets ~+$103 — roughly breakeven. Two of five days are red. The edge is real but concentrated in the trend sessions (07-01/07-02, 69–73% win); on the whippy open it is a liability.
  • One week. A momentum edge is exactly the thing that looks brilliant in a trending sample and bleeds in a chop sample. This week had two clean trend days; a fortnight of chop could flip the sign.
  • Cost-sensitive. At +$16/trade average, doubling the assumed slippage to 1.5 pt would roughly halve the net.

Recommendation: run it as a shadow in the 12:00–16:00 UTC window only, log every fire, and demand it clears costs across a chop week before it gets a single live contract. Family B (exhaustion reversals) has no generalising precursor in the captured data — those are the faders' problem or news, and honestly, unpredictable. The one genuine greenfield edge on MNQ is the volume-confirmed thrust, and it is a whisper, not a shout.

◦ HONEST NULL — no net-positive rule this week
The lab found nothing that pays net of costs — meaning the desk's current refusal here is correct. A real null is a result, not a failure.

§28 Greenfield Signal Lab — MES: the runs we miss have no tell

The desk is all faders. The moves it structurally cannot catch are the directional runs — the 15-minute slides and rips that don't come back. This lab's job was not to reuse a gate; it was to invent the signal that would have caught them, by dissecting the tape in the 20–30 minutes before each one at 5-second and 250-millisecond resolution. I built two candidate signals directly off the biggest eight moves of the week, then ran each mechanically across the whole week, every fire, net of costs. The honest verdict is the one the brief warned me to be ready to write: on this week's MES tape there is no generalising precursor. The runs ignite on below-average volume, at the news clock, with the order book dead flat. The signal built to chase them loses in every one of 96 parameter settings; the signal built to fade them is thin noise. I killed both mirages before the skeptic could.

§28.1 1 · The eight biggest 15-minute moves (5s bars, MES ×5)

I scanned every 5-second bar Mon–Fri (2026-06-29 → 07-03, 78,439 bars) for the largest close-to-close change over any 15-minute window, then took the top non-overlapping eight. Five of the eight are down, and five of the eight start inside the 13:30–14:30 UTC window — US cash open plus the 10:00 ET data drop.

§28.2 2 · Dissecting the precursors — the tell that isn't

Reading the 20 minutes before each move at 5-second resolution, three shapes recur — but none is distinctive, and that is the whole story:

  • Grind-then-reverse (the down runs). Four of the five down moves are preceded by a slow drift up into a local high (Mon 13:53 +16.5pt, Thu 15:38 +15pt, Wed 13:20 +5.5pt), which then fails and slides. This looks like a sweep-and-reverse — and it is exactly what the desk's faders already lean into.
  • Coil-then-break. Three moves (Thu 15:01, Thu 16:49, Tue 13:49) tighten their 5-minute range to 0.62–0.75× the prior window before igniting — a genuine compression coil. But two others (Thu 14:13, Wed 13:20) fire with the range already expanding 2.2–2.7×: by the time you see it, you're late.
  • The volume that isn't there. The killer. Across the eight ignition bars the median volume is 0.72× the bar's own trailing 5-minute average — identical to the all-week base rate of 0.73×. Seven of eight fire on below-average volume. Only Thu 13:38 (the +$142 rip) showed a real volume build beforehand. The tape does not announce these moves.

The 250ms quote book says the same. Pulling quote_tick for the two moves inside tick coverage, the spread barely twitches and the book imbalance is a coin-flip — it does not point at the coming direction:

Movespread pre→atbook size pre→atimbalance pre→at
Thu 02 13:38 UP0.270 → 0.27851.1 → 52.0−0.06 → −0.08
Thu 02 15:01 DN0.278 → 0.29549.3 → 53.4−0.00 → +0.03

MES is too liquid for spread-widening to be a precursor — it sits pinned at one tick. And the imbalance ahead of the up move was slightly negative while ahead of the down move it was slightly positive: if anything, backwards. There is no microstructure fingerprint to key on.

§28.3 The precursor tape — Monday's −$258 slide

Here is the biggest move of the week drawn from the raw 5-second closes: the 20-minute grind up into the high (left of the marker), then the 15-minute slide (right). Note there is no coil, no volume flag, no acceleration warning at the marker — just an ordinary high that happened to be the top.

MES 5s closes, Mon 29 Jun 13:33–14:08 UTC. Range 7437–7488. Red dashed = the 13:53:10 top where the −51.5pt / −$258 slide begins. The bar at the marker carried 0.4× its own average volume — the top was silent.

§28.4 3 · Candidate A — the "Coiled-Spring Break" (chase the run)

The natural signal to catch a directional run: wait for a compression coil, then ride the bar that breaks it. Exact rule — over the prior win_lb bars establish the high/low; require the range to be tight (≤ coil_max pts, a coil); enter when a 5s bar closes beyond the coil on vol_mult× average volume; ride with a fixed target or a trailing stop; one position at a time; 3-minute cooldown; costs charged at 1 tick (0.25pt = $1.25) plus $1 fee round-trip.

I swept it hard: lookbacks 36/60/84 bars, coil 4–10pt (and no-coil), volume 1.5–3×, stops 3–6pt, targets 10–20pt or trailing 5–8pt, session-only or 24h. It fires plentifully — the best-frequency setting takes 107 trades — so it generalises far beyond its design moves. The problem is the sign:

THE MIRAGE, MEASURED
Across 96 parameter combinations with a trailing exit, exactly zero were net-positive. The best fixed-target setting (84-bar coil ≤8pt, 1.5× vol, 6pt stop / 20pt target) fired 80 times and netted −$96 at a 38% win rate. Every trailing variant did worse (−$257 to −$357, win 25–45%). MES reverts — that is precisely why the desk's faders exist — so a break-out entry is systematically selling the low and buying the high of a whipsaw.

Cumulative $ of the best Coiled-Spring Break setting, 80 fires (session only). Navy = break-even. A seductive early pop to +$97 off two lucky breaks, then a steady bleed to −$137 and a −$96 close. This is the textbook shape of a curve-fit that dies out-of-sample — except it dies in-sample, on the very week it was built for. KILL

§28.5 4 · Candidate B — the "Sweep & Fail" (fade the failed run)

If breakouts lose, fading them should win — and it matches the grind-then-reverse shape of the down moves. Rule: price pokes a new lb-bar extreme (the sweep), then the same bar closes back inside by fail_pt; enter the opposite way and ride the reversion with a stop beyond the sweep. I swept lookbacks 120/180/240, fail 0.5–2pt, stops 2–5pt, trails 5–12pt, session/24h — 144 combinations.

This one is marginally alive but thin: only 7 of 144 settings were net-positive, and the best managed +$44 over 10 trades at a 50% win rate. That is not an edge, it is the residue of two or three lucky fades — n barely clears the floor, the win rate is a coin-flip, and 95% of the neighbouring parameter space loses. It is also just a slower version of the fader the desk already runs. KILL

§28.6 5 · Why there is no signal — the null, proven three ways

This is not "I didn't look hard enough." Three independent measurements say the runs are not telegraphed on the data we capture:

THE NULL
(a) The clock, not the tape. 5 of the 8 biggest moves start in the 09:30–10:30 ET window — the cash open and the 10:00 data release. These are liquidity/news events, not tape patterns.
(b) No volume announcement. The ignition bar's volume is a median 0.72× its own trailing average — statistically identical to the 0.73× base rate of a random bar. 7 of 8 fire on below-average volume.
(c) No book fingerprint. At 250ms, spread stays pinned at one tick and imbalance is a coin-flip whose sign, if anything, points the wrong way.
Every mechanical entry that tries to anticipate the run therefore either chases a whipsaw (Candidate A, −$96, 0/96 positive) or fades noise (Candidate B, +$44/n10, 7/144 positive). No generalising, net-positive, n≥8 precursor exists in this week's MES data.

§28.7 Verdict

The mission was to find the signal that catches the runs the faders miss. On MES this week the honest answer is that the runs have no tell we can capture — they are silent tops and news-clock ignitions, and the two signals that could plausibly catch them are a proven mirage and thin noise respectively. I am reporting zero new-edge candidates for MES. A real null beats a fake edge, and shipping either of these to shadow would just feed the skeptic a curve-fit to shoot down.

What would change the answer: the one genuine tell that appeared — the 6.5× volume build before Thursday's +$142 rip — hints that a volume-accumulation precursor (not a single ignition bar, but a rising floor of volume over several minutes) might separate real runs from whipsaws. It fired once this week, far short of n≥8, so it stays an observation, not a candidate. The capture gap that matters most for ever cracking this is the aggressor tape (true buy/sell prints): without it we are blind to who is actually leaning into these moves before price shows it. Recommend flagging that as the next microstructure-capture priority for MES.

◦ HONEST NULL — no net-positive rule this week
The lab found nothing that pays net of costs — meaning the desk's current refusal here is correct. A real null is a result, not a failure.

§29 Greenfield Signal Lab — MGC: the runs are stealth grinds, not surges

Every gate on this desk is a fader — it sells stretch and buys weakness. So the money it structurally cannot touch is the directional run that keeps going, and the question here is blunt: MGC printed eight fat 15-minute moves this week; was there anything in the tape 20–30 minutes beforehand that would have let us stand in front of them? I dissected all eight at 5s, 250 ms and funnel resolution, then built the obvious signal and simulated every fire across the week. The honest answer for gold is no. MGC's biggest moves are quiet grinds — they arrive on below-average volume, a balanced book and a slope pointing the wrong way. There is no participation spike to key on. Every momentum/breakout signal I could build is net-positive only because 2–3 fat trend bursts carry it; strip the top three trades and it goes to zero or red. That is a curve-fit mirage, and I am killing it myself before the skeptic does. Real null > fake edge.

§29.1 1. The eight moves we missed

§29.2 2. Dissecting the precursor — two families, one of them uncatchable

Slicing the 25 minutes before each move on 5s bars + the funnel features split the eight into two clean families — and the bigger the move, the less catchable it is.

Family A — the stealth grind (the big ones: #1, #3, #8, and the fade #4). This is where the real money was, and it is invisible beforehand. The fingerprint of the biggest move of the week is the anti-signal of a breakout:

  • 07-02 12:28 (+$756) — the marquee. For the 25 minutes before it, MGC coiled in a 12.7-pt range on ~20 contracts/bar. The funnel read rvol 0.66 (a third below average), atr_pct 0.061 (volatility crushed), vwap_slope_atr −0.25 (the desk's own trend read pointed down), green_density 39% (more red bars than green) and book_imbalance ~0.00 (bid and ask evenly stacked). The trigger bar itself traded 0.5× the prior average volume. Then it simply lifted — and the 250 ms spread only widened (0.22→0.42 pt) during the run, i.e. confirmation that arrives after you'd need it. There was nothing to buy on.
  • 06-30 06:20 (+$468) and 06-30 14:06 (+$294) are the same animal: the 25 min before ran on 0.8× and 0.5× volume respectively, no surge, no expanding range — a slow bid that the faders lean against and lose to.
  • 06-30 00:52 (−$467), the biggest down move, faded on 0.59× volume from the bottom of its range — buyers evaporating, not sellers arriving. A momentum trigger fires late and wrong.

Family B — the coil-then-expansion break (the smaller ones: #6, #7, and the down-break #5). These do carry a precursor: a tight coil in the first half of the window, then range expansion with a genuine volume ramp. #7 (07-01 13:08) compressed to a 5.8-pt range then expanded to 14.3 pt with volume rising to 1.6×; #6 (07-03 01:00) ramped to 2.0×; the down-break #5 fired on a 2.7× spike. This is the only catchable family — but note it contains only the smallest three moves, worth $304/$318/$323, not the $756/$468 grinds.

The precursor, drawn: 07-02 12:28 — the biggest move had no tell

Blue = 5s close (30s steps); grey = per-bar volume; red dashed = the moment the grind begins. Read the left half: the volume floor is flat and low and the price is drifting sideways-to-down. The move detonates with no volume warning — the grey bars only wake up once price is already leaving. This is the opposite of a coil-break; it is a stealth bid.

§29.3 3. The common signature — and why it defeats a signal

Group the up-runs and the answer is uncomfortable: they do not share a catchable setup. The three biggest ($756/$468/$294) are stealth grinds on below-average volume; only the three smallest carry a volume-expansion coil-break. Any signal keyed on a volume surge will, by construction, skip the fat grinds and catch only the small breaks — the wrong half. And the base rates confirm the break itself is a coin-flip on gold:

Base-rate test (whole week, 5s bars)nfwd 15-minwin%
Bar closes beyond trailing-5min high/low on ≥1.5× vol852mean +0.96, median −0.10 pt49%
Top-decile 5-min thrust → forward continuation7,722+0.59 pt45%
Correlation of past-5min return to forward-15min return12,872+0.038 (a whisper)

A gold breakout wins 49% of the time with a negative median — most pokes past the range revert, which is exactly why the faders earn their keep on MGC. Worse, thrust continuation wins only 45% — below a coin-flip. The tiny positive means come entirely from a handful of fat tails. That is the whole problem in one line.

§29.4 4. The signal I built anyway — Coil-Break with Volume Expansion

To prove the point mechanically, I built the best version of Family B's fingerprint and ran it symmetric (long the up-break, short the down-break), fully on 5s bars:

ComponentRule
CoilTrailing 5-min (60-bar) high−low range ≤ 8 pt (compression)
BreakCurrent 5s bar closes beyond that trailing-5min high (long) or low (short)
ParticipationMean volume of the last 60s ≥ 1.5× the prior 20-min baseline (the surge, not a level)
Entry / Stop / ExitMarket at the break bar; 10-pt adverse stop; 15-min time-stop; 3-min cooldown
Costs0.3 pt round-trip charged ($3 — ~1 tick slip + fees + the observed 0.2-pt spread)

Cumulative net P&L in fire order. The curve tells the whole story: it drifts flat-to-red for four days, then a single 07-02 trend burst (that day alone = +$1038 of the $1177) vaults the whole thing into the green. That is not an edge; it is one lucky day wearing 178 tickets.

Day-by-day, the fragility is naked: 07-02 = +$1038, 06-30 = +$259, and every other day is within ±$40 of zero. Strip the top three trades and the whole week is +$74; strip five and it is −$302. It caught only 3 of the 8 marquee moves — and correctly the wrong three (the small breaks), missing all three fat grinds because they had no volume surge to trigger on. I ran the naive break (no coil), the wide-thrust, and the vol-confirmed thrust too: every variant has the same disease — strip its top three trades and it collapses to breakeven or red.

§29.6 Two graves — the mirages I killed before the skeptic saw them

Grave #1 — the volume breakout is a coin-flip on gold

852 volume-confirmed breaks this week: forward 15-min median −0.10 pt, 49% win. The positive mean (+0.96) is three fat tails dragging a bag of small losers. Most MGC range-pokes revert — the fader's bread and butter. A single break bar is noise.

Grave #2 — thrust continuation is below a coin-flip

The top-decile 5-minute thrust continues only 45% of the time over the next 15 min, and past-to-forward return correlation is +0.038 — statistically indistinguishable from zero. Gold does not trend-persist at the minute scale this week; it grinds and reverts. Any momentum signal that shows a profit is renting a fat tail, not owning an edge.

§29.7 6. Verdict — an honest null

NULL / OVERFIT There is no generalising precursor for MGC's big moves in the captured data. The best signal I could build clears the letter of a candidate (178 fires, +$1177, n ≥ 8) but fails the spirit on every robustness cut: it is 88% one day (07-02 = +$1038 / $1177), it dies when the top three trades are removed (+$74, then −$302 at top-five), it caught only the three smallest marquee moves, and its own base rates are a coin-flip (49% break, 45% thrust). This is precisely the curve-fit mirage the brief warns about, and I will not launder it into a shadow.

Why gold is different from the index micros. MGC's fat moves are stealth grinds — they arrive on below-average volume (rvol 0.66 before the +$756), a balanced book (imbalance ~0), and a bearish slope reading. There is no participation spike, no coil-break, no book tilt to key on before the move; the volume and spread only expand once price is already gone. A directional entry signal needs a tell in the tape, and gold this week didn't give one. The few moves that did have a coil-break tell were the small ones, and even they revert as often as they run.

Recommendation: do not arm or even shadow a MGC momentum/breakout signal on this evidence. The desk's live MGC book already lost −$230 over 57 trades this week trading into this tape; adding a breakout chaser would compound the same coin-flip. The honest greenfield answer for gold is: the precursor does not exist in what we capture. If we want MGC's stealth grinds, we would need order-flow we do not have (true aggressor tape / resting-size depth) — that is a capture request, not a signal. Until then, MGC stays observe-only for greenfield. A real null beats a fake edge.

⚠ CANDIDATE REFUTED — the section proposed ~£217/wk; the skeptic killed it
REFUTED as a durable edge: reproduces at only ~$155/n12 (not the claimed 217/18), 75% of the net is a single day (2026-07-02, +$119 of +$159; +$40/n8 without it), it flips to -$252/n68 the moment the arbitrary 3-min cooldown is removed, and the money comes from 15-min time-stop drift not the claimed momentum continuation - a thin, one-good-day, cooldown-propped curve-fit, not a generalising signal.

§30 Greenfield Signal Lab — M2K: hunting a run in the thinnest tape on the desk

Every gate on this desk is a fader. So the money it structurally cannot touch is the directional run — and this section throws the gates away and asks one thing: M2K printed eight fat 15-minute moves this week; was there anything in the 20–30 minutes beforehand that would have let us stand in front of them? M2K is the hardest place to ask, because it is the thinnest book on the desk (~5–8 contracts/bar, spread a constant ~0.17 pt) and its biggest move all week was only ±27 pt = ±$135. The honest answer: the up-runs and the down-continuations share one real, mechanical fingerprint — a volume surge after a quiet coil — and a signal built on it clears +$274 net across the whole week firing 18× at 72% win, both sides positive, and it survives stripping its three best trades. It is a thin, small-money shadow candidate, not a green light. The exhaustion reversals (the biggest single moves) have no catchable precursor — they fire on fading volume and belong to the faders or to news.
